TENDERS.

PERSONS WILLING to Contract for the supply ot any of the undermentioned articles, to be delivered free of expense at the Workhouse, Warrington, or Cottage Homes, Padgate, at such times and in such quantities as , the Guardians or their officers may require, are requested to send in tenders to me on or before 12 noon on Tuesday, the 10th September 1912, and the same will be considered by the Guardians at a meeting to be held on Wednesday the 11th September, 1912. Tenders are required for From let October, 1912, to 30th September 1913, Sweeping Chimneys. From lst.October, 1912, to 31st March, 1913. Coal and Slack Fish and Ice Clogs Groceries and Necessaries Crockery ware Butchers' Meat Boots, Shoes and Slippers Flour and Oatmeal Coffins Bread for Cottage Homes Brooms and Brushes Drapery Leather and Shoemakers' Wines and Spirits Necessaries Drugs Tailors' Goods Dressings Butter, Bacon, Cheese, Lard Soda Water Margarine Paints etc. Mackintosh Sheeting Ironmongery Sheeting Thirds Tobacco and Snuff Bottled Stout Electrical Sundries.

Separate Tenders must be sent for the Workhouse and Cottage Homes,and samples of the articles required can be seen at either Institution between the hours of 10 to 12 noon and 2 to 4 p.m. The fuel to be supplied must he according to the specification as set forth on the tender form and quota- tions must be made for delivery either to the siding adjoining the Workhouse or by cart, end by cart to the Cottage Homes. Forms of tender, and instructions to persons tender- ing (which must be strictly complied with) may be obtained from the undersigned. All accounts will be paid monthly. The Guardians do not necessarily bind themselves to accept the lowest or any tender. By order, ARTHUR BOTTOMLEY. Clerk to the Guardians. Bewsey Chambers, August 23rd, 1912.

GOOD WORK GOING ON. Ile was glad St. Austin's and town Hall wore in the 1119.1 and,thought it was a happy augury for the political future of these wards. They would show what they could do in both municipal and Parliamentary elections. They had three Conservative members for the Council in St. Austin's ward and if their team could win the cup surely they could show the Liberal party in War- rington, and more particularly the Tory partr, that they were uoing to clear the decks when they put up a candidate for that ward. He Was ashamed that War- rington was represented as it was on the Town Council. The Conservatives had 14 full-blooded councillors and six aldermen, whereas the Liberal party had only 13 counoillors and aldermen in all. lie would never be satisfied until they got a majority in the Council and it would follow that they would get in at Parliamentary elections. (Hear, hear.) He would like them in November to capture six of the Tory seats which he hoped would be contested. They had three Liberals cowing out and if tney missed one of the .six seats it would be a standing disgrace to the Liberal party. He hoped also that they would get home of the other seats. lie himself, thongh be had no particular wish to do so,

WOULD PROBABLY FIGHT an election—(applause)—and if he fought in November it was needless to say he would win—(hear, hear)— because if he set out to fight he always set out to win. (Hear, bear) He did not want to enter into municipal work for a year or so hut if he had to do so he would expect his ward committee to carry him to victory. For about thirty years we had only bad one Liberal Mayor arid it was is reflection, and a disgraceful reflection, to the Liberal party as to why they could not have that honour. If it were offered this time, he thought a Liberal Councillor or Alderman ought to accept. (Hear, hear.) They on that committee wanted the Liberal party to have some share in the Mayoral honours of the town. (Hear, hear.) He wanted them to remember that a few years ago they got in by 153 and at the next election they lost by 240. He thought that in a work- ing class constituency:like this, such a state of affairs should never be. (Hear, hear.) They ought to educate the people of Warrington as to which side their bread was buttered on. They ought to look to themselves and their own interests and the Liberal Party was the party for the working class people. (Hear. hear). He admired men like Councillor Wilkinson and Mr Crosfield who, if they thought of personal interests would not care a rap for the Liberal Party, because theirs WAI a party which was out to tax those who had got some- thing. They merged their personal interests in the well-hoing of the people of the town. (Applause). The Liberal Party had a splendid programme and they would carry it out—(hear, hear)—and toey deserved a great deal of credit for what they had done. The Tory ?Arty promised a great deal but nothing material came of their promises, whereas the Liberal Party promised and d:d things for the uplifting of the working class. The Tory Party taxed those people who had not the money to pay. tShan,e). The Government bad lost a few , by-elections—North West Manchester. (A Voice : And Crewe). say they would not obey the laws of the other seven eights ? ("No.") He hoped the Tory Party would mend their ways and follow the lead of Mr. Balfour. PETER PEACOCK CHALLENGE CUP,

FINAL OF SUCCESSFUL COMPETITION.

SPEECHES AT THE TEA.

How true is the saying "nothing venture nothing have" and we generally find that once a start is made with the object of achieving something, some tangible result accrues and we are encouraged to go on until we have secured our object in its entirety. With a view to getting the workers in the various wards more intimate and enthusiastic in the cause of Liberal- ism, the Warrington Liberal association organised early in the year, a howling tompetition in which teams representing each w ard have taken part. A keen enthusiasm has prevailed throughout the season and the con:est,which will no doubt bean annual one, has proved higbly successful all round. Of course prizes for the trundlers to strive for had to be pro- vided and, ever willing to help his party and being an ardent lover of sport, Mr. Peter Peacock generously offered a large silver challenge cup with gold watches, for the winners and gun metal watches inlaid with gold for the runners up. A climax in the competi- tion was reached on Saturday when the final game between Town Hall A and St Austins B was played on the Bank Quay green before a large attendance. The contest was a most exciting one and the teams were very evenly matched for when the last four men took up the footers, there was only an advantage of two points in favour of the St Austins team, who finally won by :-.1(1 pcints. The victors only lost on two individual games but the first eight men on the Town Hall side ran very clo-e with their opponents hence such a narrow win. The captains of both testes played well And it was only by the odd chalk in 20 ends that W Patrick the Town Hall skipper was defeated by F. Rigby. The St. Austin's man had a slight lead up to the 12th end, when the game was 10-G in his favour. Patrick then got the block, and in fur ends peeled with 10, and on the next end led 11-10. Rigby got a good double, and at, the 19th end Patrick again peeled with 12, but failed to land against his opponent, who got his final wood on the next end. The captairi of the St. Austin's. team (J E Jefferson) had far from a rosy time against T Hughes, over whom he just managed to beat by the odd chalk after 17 ends bad been contested. After peeling with six at the eighth end Hughes got the jack and with a gcod inn brought his score to 12-6 at the 13th end. That number maintained its reputation for unluckiness, at least for the Town Hall man, who lost the jack, and with the three doubles and a single the St. Austin's captain just won 13-12 after a very interesting and exciting game. A McEvoy and Seth Hunt (St. Austin's) were very evenly matched, and after Fr ends the Town Hall man pulled it off by the odd one. Sweeping wins were recorded for eithen side, end the most shockine loss was that of R Holford, who was clean plated by J Howard (St. Austin's) in nine ends. S. Moritzdorf, Friday.

"Best wishes fcr gr,nd final tn-morrow and very hearty congratulations on striking success of cup competition. CROSFIELD." Peacock, e 4, West-street. The letter which had been received by Mr. Sanders was as follows - _ .

Dear Mr. Sanders,—l sea the final in this great cup competition is to be played on Saturday, August 17th, and I write to wish you good fortune in the weather for this very important event, a grand gathering, and as thoroughly successful a final encounter as one may fairly anticipate from the promise of the earlier matches. It would be superfluous for me to again express to all our friends from the different Wards the keen appreciation I have always felt fcr this good old-fashioned game of howls, demanding es it does self- restraint as well as steadiness of purpose and determina- tion, and encouraging a sportsmanlike sense of fair play for one's opponents. lam very certain the contest will be fought out with the greatest keenness on both sides, every man doing his level best for his own credit and the credit of his Ward. I do most heartily congratulate all those who have actively associated themselves with the organisation of this splendidly successful competition. The very large number of competitors who have entered for it, and the splendid entry—is it not also a record entry ? —for the main which you began last Saturday, is most excellent evidence of the permanent and increasing popularity of the old game,snd the strength of stalwart Liberalism in our old town. Both Mrs. Crosfleld and myself very much wish it had been possible for us to see the closing stages of this cup competition next Saturday. I Can assure you our thoughts will be with OM friends on blaturday afternoon alien I am sure excitement will run high on the green. Please drop me a line that evening and let me know the result, and give our heartiest congratulations to the winning team on their success, and to the runners-up on their victories in previous matches which have brought them so near to victory.—Yours sincerely, A. H. CROSFIELD.

BOWLING NOTES.

Warrington and District Association.

The Warrington and District Bowling Association's competition terminated with the return games on Saturday between Cemetery Hotel and Rylands, and in the consolation competition between Liberal Club and Penketh Park. The winners were Rylands and the Liberal Club who won on the home and home games by 40 and 43 points respectively.

===== 0002601_19120824_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
CEMETERY HOTEL v. RYLANDS.

The wire-WO-lie-I.—s -ge-in-e-d—r-ta;:b.siaaiai-aliv—arirre on the Gorsey Lane Green when the first game was played and although on their own green on Saturday the Cemetery men knew they could not bring up their scores to win, but nevertheless they all fought hard and they reduced the win of Itylunds from one of 57 points to one of 40. The wireworkers only won on four individual games but the other players ran close to keep matters fairly even.

One of the best, if not the best, garne of the after- noon was that between G Ball, jun. (Cemetery) and W Roughley. The players kept fairly even on the lint ends and peeled with 11 at the 14th end atter Roughley had led 0-3 at the Bth end. Roughley got the block again and ran to 1043 hut 8411, who knows every inch of the land, soon pee'ed and at tile 26th end he only wanted one to his opponents three. Roughley however got two Dice singles and at, the 28th end the score was 20 each. Ball got (n a nice length and landed one to win by the odd chalk.

H Eyes (Rylands) played a good game against .7 Bibby and maintained a lead, although only slight, up to the 19th end, when after getting n double the Cemetery player pot in front by 12-10, Bibby then bad a good run and at the 26th end he led by 19-13 but after three more ends he only led by two points and a good double score on the 30th end gave him the game by only four points.

After winning eight out of the first ten ends and lead- ing 10-3 Abtringer (Ryland) eventually lost to A Scott by seven chalky in 26 ends. Scutt played a winning game well and got well on the jack almost every time on the last hi ends.

H Williams (Rylands) was assuredly the favourite whey, in his game with W J Wood he soon ran up a score of 16 9 but after adding two more singles at the 20th and 21st ends he fell away and, securing the block, Wood played a good length with keen judgment and eventually won by 21-18.

The other games were fairly well contested and the Wireworisers have ahown good form throughout the eettaon.

Consolation Competition. LIRAI, CLUB V. PENKETII PARK The green at Penketh on Saturday was in a rather heavy state hut nevertheless the villagers set out to give the visitors a warm time of it and after many good individual games they reduced the Liberal Club's lead to one of 43 points ftom over eighty.

The Liberals only won on three individual games but all played well and they are justly proud of their achievement, for on the first game at the Liberal Club it will be remembered they won on every individual game.

J Firth (Liberal) w ho won by such a narrow majority over Jtif Bowyer in the first match was up against J Lloyd and taated the hitternees of defeat by only one point. One would have thought be WB3 in for a good win for at the 26th end he lea 19.15 but Lloyd played his woods well to peel with 19 at the 30th end. After two more ends the snore was again equal,each man only wanting one for game. The excitement ran high when the jack was sent up and the Penkethites gave a lusty cheer when Lloyd got one in to win.

A good game was witnessed between T O'Brien (Liberal) and John Bowyer for they peeled at 8 and then O'Brien got away and led 15-10 at the 19th end. Bowyer then ran the little 'on and at the 2lth end had an advantage of 17-15. O'Brien made his final score on the next end when he was 17 against 18 and with three singles Bowyer ran ta game.

-- W Shave (Penketh) had far from a sweet thing on in his game with A Gleave for the Liberal bowled well although he lost by three points after 27 ends. Toe players kept fairly even and peeled with 9, 11,13,15 and at the 15th end the game was 18 each. A double and a single on the next two ends gave Shave the game.

A similar game was played between H Glover (Panketh) and ti Dainteth and the score was equal on many occasions, and there was never a vast diflerence between the contestants. After peeling with 16 at the 2nd end Glover seemed to fall away, and only recorded another chalk whilst Daintith ran to game on the 28th end by 21-17.

-- F Walker (Liberal) gained a praiseworthy victory over S Glover by 21-13 in 25 ends. His superiority was more marked in the latter stages of the game, when be had three doubles in three ends to his credit.

-- One is glad that thA honour of winning this competi- tion has come to Warrington and more particularly that the Liberal Club team are the champions, because they have stuck to their task with marked tenacity.

PROPERTY OWNERS' OUTCRY.

STREET-MAKING TOO EXPENSIVE.

OWNERS " BROUGHT TO THE VERGE OF RUIN."

The pace at which the work of paving streets and passages is proceeding in Warrington is said to press heavily on the property owners and they made a strong appeal for more favourable terms at the IJOCIIi Govern- ment Board inquiry held at the Town Hall, on Tuesday morning, by Mir. Efgar Dudley, The subject of the itquiry was the application of the Corporation for sanction to borrow loans of £11,223 for works of the private street improvement and £1,777 for the purchase of land for street improvement purposes. Considerable interest was taken in the proceeoings, among those present being :—Aldermen Smethurst, Pierpoint and James Evans, Councillors Pemberton, Birtles, Jackson Holmes and Henshall, Messrs A T Hallaway (deputy town clerk),J Fairhurst (borough tressurer),T Longdin (horough surveyor), A M Ker (assistant surveyor), W Throssell, F W Wright, G T ilding, E England and P Lawton.

DROUGHT TO TIIN VERGE OF RUIN

end in some instances property had been seized. They, therefore, made those requests for a cheaper form of street-making and for an extended period of repayment. In their opinion they ought to hsve 14 years for repayment. They agreed that the main roads should be of the best possible description, but side streets and hack passages might reasonably be constructed in a cheaper way. Tne Borough Surveyor said his impression of the tar macadam system was that it was ut comparattvely recent introduction and hid not had a chance. He did not suppose it would last more than twelve years at the outside, and then the ratepayers would have to bear the expense of repairing it. The scavenging would not be so satisfactory. Then the road was only an artificial one, whereas the sett paving was permanent. Mr Wright (to the Surve3or) : You have seen the streets at Stockton Heath P—Yes.

And are you aware that the contractors gave a guarantee that they would keep them in order for five years ?—I am not. The Borough Surveyor remarked that the last ques- tion was exactly his point—that of maintenance. . . . .

Mr Lawton : "Are you aware that the West of London is paved in this way, and many of the county boroughs have adopted it ? It seems peculiar that Warrington is so full of knowled•ge,yet all these large cities and towns are doing this particula, thing that we are requesting should te done.

Councillor Pemberton said that although the contrao- tors had guaranteed to maintain the Stockton Heath roads for live years, that dii not say that that was the life of the roads. Southport was an important town— quite as important as Warrington—and he thought its streets and pavings would be very hard to beat from the point of view of sanitation and durability. Tar macadam had been adopted there. If it was laid at the proper time it would stand as well as any road. It was a hardship upon property-owners that there should be those expensive specifications for unimportant streets. Some ot the hack„streets were oul de sacs and the only vehicles which used them were hawkers' carts. The Warrington Corporation had wakened up recently in regard to the making of private streets, and it bad borne very heavily upon property-owners. Be had known a number of instances where people had bad to dispose of their property ; they had had to practically give it away, as he was reminded by Comicilior Henshall. They could not get fair prices for it. The Deputy Town Clerk: Perhaps Councillor Hen- shall will also tell you that some Impsrial considers- SiODS have had to do with that, I think, as well. Councillor Pembetton : I could say a great deal on that it you will allow me. The Deputy Town Clerk : It is hardly fair to blame tha Warrington Corporation for the present ct premien of the property market in the country. Couociflor Pemb,rton : I am referring to cases of what happened before that. You can't give property away now. He added that wherever the Corporation required property it always had to pay a bigger price than an ordinary person, and then the vendors did not think they got half enough for it. There was a great cry all over the country about the dearth of working men's dwellings, and no wonder. Rising rates and the increasing demands of health authorities made the life of property owners almost unbearanle.The tar macadrm roads at Stockton Heath were wearing extremely well. From actual personal experience he knew that the Board bad allowed a term of repayment of ten years. The Deputy Town Clerk said that the last loan granted by the Board for the Runcorn District Council b. as for seven years. Councillor Pemberton : lam paying for a term of ten years. I don't know how on earth they can give ten years and only receive seven. The Inspector suggested unofficially that it might appear as reasonable to ask the builder of a house to give ten years for the repayment of his account as to risk the Corporation to allow that long for street- making accounts. Councillor Pemberton said that the difference was that the Corporation were the servants of the rate- payers. Continuing, he said he was not merel; asking for ten years, but for 14 years. In his capacity as vice- chairman of the Street Improvement Committee, it was one of the most unpleasant. duties he had to perform to vote for the making of the streets, although it' WO-S in the public interests. He had felt he had been doing a cruel thing in many instances. Several working men, who, by the exercise of thrift, hal invested in property, had had to dispise of it at a loss. He could not see that anybody would be injured by granting a term of 2.0 years. Then the specification was too expensive. The Inspector : Why cannot you persuade 300 r Council to alter the specill2ation ? Councillor PEMBEItTON : I don't know whether you have had any experience of the Council. (Laughter.) They want a bit of persuading ! But, unfortunately, the whole of the members don't take a sufficient interest in the work of each committee. That is the difficulty of public management. It the whole of the Council knew what the Paving and Sewerage Committee know, 'they would no doubt make an alteration. The Deputy Town Clerk : I think you will be very much interested to know that Councillor Pemberton is the deputy-chairman of the committee dealing with these matters, and he seconded the minutes at the Council meeting approving of the streets being made in this way. Councillor Pemberton: That is a mere matter of form, as you know;. a mere technicality.

11Ir England submitted that it was absolutely unnecessary to pave the streets with setts, seeing that they were riot main streets. The Corporation had not altered its system since 1882, and the nature and volume of traffic had wonderfully changld since then. The Borough Treasurer favoured the extension of time being gray ted. At present seven years were granted oy the Board arid the Council granted seven years to the property owners, hut it was a difficult matter to fit toe payments in. The majority kept up their payments—in fact they had to do so, in order to satisfy the Board. Sometimes he was afraid they bad had to squeeze the owner, and on some ozcssions had had to take possession of the property. Councillor Flenshall referred to the fact that some of the streets to be made up were cul-de.sacs, wherein the foot of man never trod—excer, the nightsoil men oncel a week Yet the specifications for these permanent cul-de-sacs were the same as for high roads where there was uninterrupted traffic. It was obvkusly not necessary to pave permanent cul-de-sacs, which were scarcely ever used, with the same materials with which highways were paved. With regard to the question of secondary streets, the Corporation was probe oly making progress towards reform. A resolution had been passed on the point, and it might be that they had got in the thin edge of the wedge or were at the hreaking of th dawn. _

The Deputy Town Clerk : The breaking of the set is what 3ou want. (Laughter). Councillor Herishall continuing, said he did not wish to parade the sufferings of the small property owners. It was a matter of tragedy in the cases of scores of property owners in Warrington. Men had saved up perhaps OM or so, and in many cases the ciaity had been absolutely swept away and the

might be pardoned tor thinking he would have been better off if he had spent his money in drinK or tour- ing on the Continent. (Laughter.) He had had some strange reflections on "bmiles' self-help"in the last few years. (Laughter.) However, the Corporation had their duty to perform, and they had tried to du it as considerately as possible. Mr. Lawton objected to the loan on the ground that the estimated cost of paving was too great. The Inspector : The Board cannot dictate to the Corporation as to paving. Do you wish the Biard to refuse sanction to the loan?

Mr Wright: We hardly wish that, sir. The Inspector : All your remarks would seem so much more tl the point if addressed to the Council. Mr Wright: We thought an expression ot opinion from von might help the paving Committee. Mr 'England : We feel that the paving should go on for the sake of the health of the corn nunity. At the same time, if you suggested that a cheaper method of paving secondary streets should be adopted, it might carry weight. That is really one of our points, and the extra time for repayment. Air Lawton said that what the inspector had suggested had been done. A deputation had waited upon the Paving end Sewetage Committee. He had sent in an objection to the plying, hut had received DO reply. They felt that if the Inspector sugsested the cheaper method the Council would be strengthened, and the opposition might get what they required. Street making had never been carried on at the rate it was being done to-day. It seemed likely that streets would be made in cases where there were no houses.

The Deputy Town Clerk replied to the observations of the deputation from the Property Owners' Associa- tion. He said the Corporativn were considering the question of cheaper paving, and were obtaining statistics from other towns. Ile was not able to pro- duce them at the inquiry,but those they had got showed that the almost universq custom was to adapt the s:tt pa- ing. The Corpzra im felt that they ivre in a peculiar position. They were the servants of the whole of the ratepa3ers, and not of any individual body. If the paving was carried out in the way suggested the Council were advised that they would be putting an unfair burden upon the genera body of ratepayers, inasmuch es they would have to repair the ttreets much sooner than would other wise be the case. The Corporation were , trying as far '..as passible to strike a happy medium between the rights of the general body of rLOpase's, at.d the individual. They felt that if they acceded to the requests they would te putting too great a burden upon the general body. In Warrington the r.,perty owner was treated much be, ter than In a great many other Lancashire tuwns. To hear Councillor Pemberton decliim upon Southport in the Council Chamber was quite different from hearing him speak at that inquiry. He told them in the Council Chamber when speaking of the smoke nuisance, that Southport was a seaside resort and Warrington vas a manufacturing town, (Laughter). There was a great diff-rence between the traffic in a manufacturing town and that in a seaside resort.

inquiry then closed.

PAVING TO LAST 100 YEARS.

The Deputy Town Clerk said the period asked for repayment in respect of the £1,777 for Silver-Areet improvement was 60 years, and for the private street works ten years. They bad asked for the latter period for private street works at nearly every inquiry for the last four or five years, but had not as yet been successful in obtaining it. Twit morning there would be a deputation from people who were principally interested in the paving of streets—the property owners—who would present the case for an extension ' of the repayment period. The Corporation heartily agreed with that proposition. At present only seven years were allowed. If it WAS intended to put down a paving that was only going to last seven years or some short period, the principle upon which the Board acted in respect of repayment could be understood, bat the Council put down pavements which were calculated to last for at least fifty years. In S .nkey-street tnere was a pavement which had been down for fifty years, and it was as good to-day as when it was put down. The Borough Surveyor would say that the system of paving that they bad carried out would last at least 100 years, and he bad based the application for an increased term of years upon the good, solid and permanent system of street making which the Corporation adopted. THE VALUE OF PROPERTY IN WARRINGTON. Dealing with the application of £1,777 for Silver. street property, the Deputy Town Clerk explained that the Corpoetion had purchased two lots of property The Council for some time had had a definite scheme, which on different occasions had been approved by the Local Government Board, for the wideninc el Winwick- road, Pinners-brow, and Silver-street Pinners-brow was a narrow entrance to a main road leading out of the borough. One lot of propery-5. 7, and 9 Winwick-road, 1 to 7, Old Gardens, and a cottage at the rear ot Witiwica-road—was purchased from Mrs. Crompton at a public auction for £1,505. It had an area of 1,071 square yards. Roughly the price was 303. per square yard. The gross rental was £139 Is. The Inspector observed that £1,505 seemed a large price to give at a public auction. On what basis was it paid ? The Deputy Town Clerk : The only basis I Can suggest is that the Corporation used a price of £1,500, and the property went for L 1.505. The Inspector : I have not seen this property, but I think if the owner got £5O per year out of it he was doing fairly well. The Deputy Town Clerk : I don't say the property Is cheap at all. The Inspector : It is 30 years' purchase if my suggested figure is correct. The Borough Surveyor stated that the vice per sqllare yard was 28s. Id. The Deputy Town Clerk next pointed out that Winwick-road would be widened to the full width of ,00 feet. Its .present width at the point under notice was about 27 feet. Another lot of property purchased from Mrs Brown, numbered 2 and 4, Pinners-brow was 1112 .square yards in area, and cost £255. The gross rental was e 2.2 23. This property was a!so bought at a public auction. The Inspector : It is not the usual thirty years' purchase. The Deputy Town Clerk : It is not the practice in Warrington to give thirty years' purchase. The Inspector I have bad two or three experiences. The Deputy Town Clerk said the proper standard value of property in Warrington at the present time was one fixed at a recent aroitratiou at about 22 years' purchase, including the usual ten per cent. That was shop property. People were anxious to use chance observations at inquiries as furthering their case. The Inspector : I hope they don't take my observa- tions as.justification of a 30 years' purchase in cottage property. The Deputy Pawn Clerk : lam afraid we have had it thrown up against us very often. I think it would be just as well to say publicly—and, with great respect I suggest that possibly my observations might carry as much weight on the other side—that the proper value of property in Wsrrington in the main streets is about 221 years' purchase. The Inspector: I hope nobody ran away with the idea that I thought it snould be 30 years purchase. The Deputy Town Clerk : I am afraid some mis- guided people may attempt to make capital out of it. Air EL England : The Deputy Town Clerk is only referring ton recent local valuation. Yours is evidently national experience. The Inspector : It is, as far as England and Wales are concerned. The Deputy Town Clerk further explained that within a quarter of a mile of the property were 28 empty houses available for people who had occupied the houses involved in the purchase. Accommodation was thus provided for 154 persons, whereas under forty would be displaced. Consequently there would be no displacement as contemplated under the Housing of the Working Claeses Act. There wee no objection to the application, which was closed.

NEW SYSTEM OF STREET-MAKING SUGGESTED.

THE WOES OF THE PROPERTY OWNER.

It was in the application to borrow £11,223 for Private street works that interest chiefly centred, and representatives of the property owners not only put in a plea for a longer period for the repayment of loans, hut strongly advocated a cheAper system of making the less important streets and pas..ages. These, it wos con- tended, might be treated just .s effectively with tar macadam end aspbalt as with setts, and the 'cost would be considerably lower. The Deputy Town Clerk said the application referred to over 73 different streets and properties. The Cor- poration had visited the whole of the streets and con- sidered it necessary to pave them. He understood there was to objection to the question of paving. Some objections to the apportionments had been received and were in process of settlement. He anticipated that they would all be settled without the aid of the Court, with one exception. The Borough Surveyor gave evidence as to the pro.

posed specification for the making of the streets, providing for sett paving. In his opinion setts, both from the point of view of scavenging and durability, were the cheapest method of paving. He did not think any town in Lancashire did this kind of work cheaper than Warrington did. Mr. F W Wright. on behalf of the Property Owners' Association, said he was not there to object to the making of the streets, nor was the deputation present in any spirit of hostility to the Corporation, but it was thought the time bad arrived when some consideration should be granted to persons called upon to pave ths streets, and to relieve them somewhat of the enormous burdens that they had to tear. Although, as the Borough Surveyor bad pointed out, the streets were to be paved in accordance with a resolution of the Coun- cil, the time bad arrived when some deviist ion from that resolution might reasonably be allowed. Properly coo- structecl tar-macadam roads with asphalt tops were quite sufficient for sire streets and back pass- ages. That method if constructing streets bad paL•seci beyond the experimental stage. It was being adopted throughout the country, and had been tried in the suburbs of ‘Varringron with very gratifying results. The reason the objections were being made was on the ground of unnecessary burdens being forced upon property owners. As regarded the life of the form of street which be advocated, it would bear favourable comparison with the life of sett paving. From a sanitary point of view also tar macadam would be far better. The rate of progress of street making iii Warrington had been somewhat rapid, and was press-

lag heavily upon small property-owners, and having regard to that fact he made a strong appeal to the BJarct to grant an extended period for the repayment of the loans. In a neighbouring district where the tar mam.darn system had been adopted, the Board had allowed a term of ten years. The Inspector : I wit i make a note of that. lam rather surprised to Inar you sly to. Mr. Wright mentioned that that term had been allowed at Stockton Heath. It had been a very dlfiuult matter for many property owners to keep pace with the rate of progress In street-making. A HANDY BLOW-LAMP.

• The following is a method of constructing a very handy blow-lamp, which will be found to be better than the ordinary blow-lamp, because the flame can be regulated until it is almost as fine as a needle. It gives a better and steadier flame, and will be found very useful to those who cannot blow with the mouth very well. Take two pieces of lain. brass tube A and B about 4in. long. Turn fcur plugs to fit tightly into the ends of these tubes, and solder them in securel7. Drill and tap a un. hole in one of the plugs, and turn up and screw a- piece of brass c to fit, with a On. hole to carry the wick. Drill

and tap a in. hole in on _ Lne plugs of the other tube. Then turn and screw a piece of 1:i0. brass to iin. to fit the last hole, leaving a flange D about thick. Drill a hole through it to take a fin. copper tube. Bend the copper tube E to the shape shown in Fig. 1, and braze a plug in the end. Drill a tin. hole in the tube, and braze a piece of brass in it. Then drill and tap a lin. hole it. the brass. Turn up a fin. brass ball F, leaving a kin. spindle, which must be screwed to fit tightly into the brass -plug (see Fig. 2). Through the brass ball drill and tap a No. 2 B.A. bole, and drill a 1-32 in. hole through th.:• spindle to meet this hole. Turn s No. 2 B.A. screwed plug G, which must fit very tightly into the ball. The-point of this plug must be tapered off, and the head can be left square. -

&TM 'a piece of tin. b.mss No. 2 B.A. about i,in. long, file a square on it, and turn the other end a slight taper H. Through this brass drill a l-32in. hole up to l-64in. of the point, and knock the point of a very fine needle through the 'remainder; the finer the hole the better the results. Countersink the hole at the screwed end to form the seating for the regulating valve. Solder the other end of the copper tube intc the hole in the in. screwed brass. Make tvvN., leather washers to go under the tube ano the other wick carrier, and two clips to g< round the brass cyinders, to keep then together as shown. Force a piece of wick up the copper tub' 1,5 far as it w4:l go, leaving enough to read

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY AUGUST 24, 1912.

if the Parliamentary THE ULSTER correspondent of the PLOT. " Times " is to be credited, the "solemn , covenant" which is to be taken by the anti- Home Rulers of Ulster on September 28th is to pledge the signatories that if the Home Rule Bill becomes law they will not acknow- ledge the new Parliament in Dublin, obey its laws, or pay its taxes. To that " covenant " it appears will be added a declaration by the Ulster Unionist Council of the scheme for the Provisional Government which they propose to set up in the north-eastern counties of Ulster contingently on the passage of a Home Rule Bill. This scheme will include a separate judiciary and an Assembly. The threat to set up a Provisional Government has always appeared to us to be of the most serious char- acter. It is not the first time that such a proposal has been made in Ireland. If we recollect aright, the crime for which ROBERT EMMET. the Irish patriot, was executed more than a hundred years ago was precisely this of proposing to set up a Provisional Govern- ment in defiance of that existing. It may be that in the present case the leaders of the Ulster Unionists rely upon the fact that the Government which they propose to defy does not yet exist to protect them from immediate consequences. That is a matter which the legal advisers of the Crown will, of course, have to consider and to settle. We do not pretend to be able to decide the precise moment at which action of the kind proposed by the Ulster Council becomes illegal and in- dictable; but that it does so ultimately if carried into effect is obvious. It must be assumed that the Ulster leaders are not in- sensible to the very grave responsibility

which, apart from their character as mem- bers of his Majesty's Privy Council (to which many of them belong), they are incurring. The pledge which is to be taken binds its subscribers to refuse obedience to the laws of the Dublin Parliament, and to refuse to pay its taxes. The practice of tax-resisting seems to be catching. Certain individuals have undertaken the task of refusing to pay the " tax " imposed by the Insurance Act in this country, and they have been dealt with effectively by the law. But what are the taxes which the Ulstermen will resist ? The Dublin Parliament, under the Home Rule Bill as it stands at present, will not collect taxes other than duties of postage. That func- tion will be performed, as hitherto, by the Treasury of the United Kingdom. It will, therefore, be seen that refusal to pay taxes will not be defiance of the Dublin Parlia- ment at all but defiance of the Imperial Parlia ment, and refusal to respond to the demands of the collectors of the Imperial Exchequer. In that respect, then, the strategic position of the Ulster Council is a false one from the outset. But, whatever weak points may, be found in the plan of the Ulster leaders, one thing must be understood. It is thatthe defiance of the authorityof Parlia ment cannot be tolerated. The " Times " in discussing the question, seems to think that because a large proportion of the wealth c)f Ireland resides in Ulster, the leaders of this embryonic rebellion cannot be brought to account. "Rich men Rule the Law," wrote a great Irishman; but reliance on that asseveration will assuredly prove a mistaken faith in the case of Ulster.

The greatest modern THE FUTURE OF apostle of sacrifice and "THE ARMY." service is dead, and the

whole world is the poorer for his loss. So much is acknowledged in the tributes which have poured into Eng- land from all quarters of the globe. General BOOTH was a world figure controlling and inspiring a world organisation. and his removal from this life leaves Christendom without one of its most potent agents. The Salvation Army has passed through the fire of bitter criticism and opposition. It has withstood the fierce assaults and savage gibes of those who found the parallel for its methods in the frenzied ritual of the Corybantes. The cold indiffer- ence of the Churches has failed to put barriers to its progress, and to-day it is accorded a place among the most potent and powerful of Christian agencies. How far the Army was dependent upon its head remains to be seen. General BOOTH had ceased to be as was in its early days the mainspring of all the life and energy of the Army. It had outgrown him. Its system was perfected on his lines; but, while allegiance to the GENERAL was preserved on every side, the vast "business" was run by those to whom he had deputed the various tasks. No leader was ever more carefully watched and guarded than was the I GENERAL He was at once the leader and I the servant of the gfeat body which he had created.

The strength of the Salvation Army in its earlier days lay in its power to draw to its standard the weak and the criminal. It held out hope to those whom society had abandoned. It welcomed the fallen, and used the record of their sins to win their fellows to its standard. There was no " hushing' up" of " vileness' in the programme of the GENERAL. The thief who had come to the Mercy Seat was encouraged—nay, required b shout his iniquities aloud in the public places, that others might know from what depths he had been rescued. It has often been urged against the Army that the con= versions were not permanent, that the excite- ment and emotionalism of the moment being over the sinners returned to their evil ways, confirmed in their criminality by the fame which the recital of their sins had obtained for them. Even were such a charge in general true it would not prove the Army to have failed; but the rescue of many.of the fallen, and their conversion into useful citizens, stands as an answer to the charge. In recent years, perhaps, the strength of the Army in this country has lain more on its social and less on its religious side. The 1" Hallelujah Bands" still parade the streets and penetrate into the slums, but the number of recruits they attract to the banner is said to be steadily diminishing. Revivalism is a fluctuating quantity, and the power of pure emotionalism appears to be a little spent in England at the present moment But side by side with all this is the powerful organisation for dealing with the homeless and the outcast. Salvation Army shelters and farm colonies represent a phase of work of which GENERAL BOOTH laid the founda- tions in his appeal to the humanity of his country in the pages of "Darkest England," and it may be that on this side of the Army's work will be found the element of stability. It is well that the Salvation Army has this force in its control. Christianity has to face assaults from all sides. The primitive faith to-day is being shaken, and the teachings of theology are more and more exposed to the weakening influences of" modern reason." It is by its achievements for the regeneration of the people and their social uplifting that the Christian Church as a whole will prove victorious in the end; and in that victory the Salvation Army should have no small part. Upon the secular side of national life it has worked, and is working, for causes which are at the very foundation of the future progress

of the world. That it will continue to take its part in religious and social work must be the wish of all who have regard to the welfare of those classes for whom the message of the Army was specially designed. The inspira- tion and the example of the great man who has passed beyond the Barrier connot fail to support those who are left.

For many years the WARRINGTON promoters of the War- SHOW. rington Show used to sigh for the day when really fine summer weather would crown their enterprise. Warrington show and a wet day were regarded almost as synonymous terms. But now for two years in succession, the clerk of the weather has apparently forgotten himself and the shows of 1911 and 1912 have been carried out under something approaching ideal conditions. Still it has proved impossible to shake off the bad luck which so persistently pursues the ,society. Last year it was the railway strike; on Thursday it was the foot and mouth disease which considerably curtailed the scope of the show and necessitated a change in the venue. Some day, perhaps, the society will be favoured in every respect and then we may expect the attainment of that long- waited result— a reasonable profit. Of the quality of Thursday's show, it is only necessary to state that it was up to the Walton standard—which means that in most of the classes there was but little room for improvement. The society is doing a good work in encouraging local breeders of horses and it deserves well of the agricultural community and of the public in general.

There are two remark- A PLAIN INFERENCE. able features of the

_ agitation by which Tory leaders, like Mr. Bonar Law and Sir Edward Carson, are endeavouring to intimi- date, or cajole, the British elector on the " Ulster" question. The first is the extreme coyness which the Tory Party has shown of making Home Rule the predominant issue at by-elections. They assert that the electorate was quite unaware, in December, 1910, that the Liberal Government would introduce a Home Rule Bill. But even after the intro- duction of the Bill, the Tory Party was far `..rom desiring to make Home Rule the chief issue at by-elections. At the General Election of December, 1910, the Tory Party proclaimed vociferously that Home Rule was the issue. Since the introduction of the Home Rule Bill, Tory candidates at by-elections have sedulously explained that the main issue on which they were fighting was not Home Rule. The inference is plain.

The second remarkable SHIFTING feature of the Tory 3110UNDS. Anti-Home Rule agita- tion is the uncertainty Of its ground. Sometimes they make great play with the argument, which their own electioneering tactics refute, that the country voted Liberal in 1910 in ignorance of the Government's intention to introduce a Home Rule Bill, and that the British elector is fretting and pining for the chance to condemn Home Rule at the ballot-box. Sometimes they justify threats of rebellion against a statutory Home Rule Parliament on the ground that Orange loyalists would be "forced out of the Union." Sometimes they rely upon the plea that Protestants must be protected from Catholic intolerance. Do they or do they not, admit that if the House of Commons has received an electoral man- date in favour of a Home Rule Parliament, resistance is unlawful and unpatriotic?

Mr. J. Foster Fraser is "AMERICA AT the Tariff Reform candi- Womc." date for Lincoln. If he accepts the uSual Tariff Reform doctrine that Protection is a good thing, we would like to ask him whether he found it a good thing for the workers in the United States when he was there. The " Lincoln Leader" recalls certain passages in Mr. Foster Fraser's book, 'America at Work." Let us repeat them : "Where are your elderly workmen ?" I asked a Philadelphia manufacturer once. With a care- less smile, he said, "Have a smoke and we'll take a car ride along to the cemetery. (Page 49). On page 144 of the same book, Mr. Fraser relates that he said to Mr. Davies, Chief Factory • Inspector for Illinois State. "You Americans are always making comparisons about the dis- tress of Europe and the fine wages made in the United States. To this, Mr. Davies, according to Mr. Fraser, replied, " I can take you to places where life is just a struggle ; where if you gave a one cent, banana to a family of five, it would be the greatest treat they had had for weeks." " Wages can only be reckoned by their purchasing power. Therefore, while the American working man undoubtedly earns more than a Briton, he has to work harder, and he has to pay more for the necessities of life—in the case of house rent about three times as much. Of recent years wages in Chicago have increased 10 per cent., but the cost of food has increased 40 per cent., so the working man is worse off than he was five years ago." (Page 198). It would be well to ask members of the Tariff Reform League to explain what they mean when they say that Protection puts an end to hunger and unemployment.

LEGACY TO THE INFIRMARY.

/The Late Mrs._Hartley's Handsome

Bequest-.

Tbe late Mrs. Elizabeth Hartley, of The Laurels, Latchford, who passed away on the 14th inst. at the age of eighty-one, has bequeathed a legacy of £5OO to the Warrington Infirmary. Mrs. Hartley was prominently identified with the Wesleyan denomina- tion and was much esteemed for her good works. It is to her generosity that the Wesleyan Church at Latchford owes its handsome organ. The bequest to the Warrington Infirmary was announced to the authorities of that institution in the following letter to the Secretary (Mr. J. H. J. Hampson). 2, Cairo-street, Warrington, 19th August, 1912. Dear Sir.—l am instructed by the executors of the late Mrs. Elizabeth Hartley, of The Laurels, Latch- ford, who died on the 14th just, to inform you that by her will Mrs. Hartley gave a legacy of £5OO free from duty- to the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary. Some short time must of necessity elapse before the Executors are in a position to pay over the legacy, but I will communicate with you again in due course. —Yours faithfully, THOMAS RIDGWAY.

LNOTES AND NOTIONS.

In the course of his interesting article (page 7) on the old Abbey of Vale Royal, Mr. A. Mounfield mentions the recently-dis- covered fact that the Abbey was the largest ever built in England. What a pity the destruction by order of Henry VIII. was so complete. * * *

The airman has been seen and conquered. The Million Penny Fund has made 345. out of the event, and Alderman Bennett has written an airy trifle. The most satisfied people (with the exception of the Alderman) are the suburbians, who had a fine view of the flying free, gratis and for nothing!

, * * After or before the hours mentioned wave movements of the air in the upper atmos- phere, the atmospheric perturbations which cause earthquakes and other natural pheno- mena, are more frequent. From eleven to five are the hours when storms, changes in the weather, and death and destruction are most in evidence. * *

Lloyd George and the Government were "catching it hot" on Thursday evening in Corporation-street, where the Tariff Reform van had made a halt. A man with a very big voice was addressing a verb small crowd. We stayed long enough to hear him recite allist of things Liberal and describe them with great emphasis as follows: "All these is either 'alf-truths or deliberate lies." Carried nem con.

We have not waited in vain. Like many other Tariff Reform inconsistencies, it comes from Birmingham. A meeting was held in the town the other day to protest against the Insurance Act. The Chairman said : It was not a British Act of Parliament at all. It was imported from Germany. . . Have we fallen so low that our Parliament is going to import Acts of Parliament ? If it comes to that, have we fallen so low that we cannot maintain our trade without adopting German methods? • •

In an article entitled "Tariff Reform," in the August number of the "Fortnightly Review," Mr. F. E. Smith, M.P., declares : There must be a tariff desirmed to help agricul- ture just as much as manufacturing industry. Manufacturers are to be " helped " by an import duty that will enable them to raise their prices. Agriculturists are to be " helped " in the same way. This is how, we suppose, Tariff Reform is to make "everything cheaper." 05 *

Poor old Warrington! For years past, cycling and motoring correspondents of various newspapers and periodicals have damned Warrington wholly and heartily for the ugliness of its main thoroughfares and the vileness of its paving. Now it appears that aviators are to follow suit in their own particular sphere. Mr. Hueks complained on Tuesday that "Flying at this place is very difficult: you can hardly see it for smoke. Several times I lost the ground." It was also very cold in the upper air, but Warring- ton escaped blame in this respect. * * *

To-day is St. Bartholomew's day which this year has special significance inasmuch as it marks the 250th Anniversary of Non- conformity. On August 24th 1662 the Ministers of the Church of England were required to declare their assent to certain ' propositions or leave their livings. Two thousand of the clergy,among them the rector of Warrington, left the Church. We ' announce in another column a series, of articles on Warrington Nonconformity in which the story will be fully told. The first will appear next Saturday. * * *

When is the best time for flying? A Russian engineer who has made a special investigation of the question writes to a Russian newspaper to say that the best hours are from five to eleven morning and after- noon. His reason for this statement is that the atmospheric pressure is at its lowest about four o'clock, and reaches its maximum after nine or ten o'clock. The engineer apparently thinks that the further he gets from the minimum, and the nearer he gets to the maximum pressure, the safer will the airman be.

- - No man knew better* how to make use of the journalism of the day to further the causes he had at heart than General Booth. He was a great advertiser of his Army, • and he wanted and got most of his advertisement free. His personal attitude towards the journalist was touched, perhaps, with more than a little of that grim humour which illuminated most of his speeches. It is related that at a big meeting in the North of England at which a number of the local pressmen made their way to the front of the meeting and took their seats on a form which seemed to have been specially reserved, the General gazed at them a moment, and then rising to his feet and leaning over the platform, he exclaimed, with a sweep of the arm which comprehended the whole bench, "That is no place for reporters; that is the penitents' form."

* * What rights has the sleeper in regard to the noisy nocturnal cat? This is a question which naturally arises from a local prosecu- tion this week by the R.S.P.C.A. The defendant had been disturbed in the small hours for the past three months by a prowl- ing cat; on two occasions it had stolen the family's Sunday dinner. Finally he threw a poker at the animal, and the shot was so good that the cat's back was broken. It then howled so loudly that the Inspector beard, and the exasperated householder was haled before the bench for cruelty and fined ss. Now if the poker had killed the cat outright probably nothing more would have been heard of the matter, and the thrower of the poker would have been looked upon locally as a public benefactor. But because the animal was only injured and not killed, the man with a real grievance becomes an offender against the law and has to pay the penalty. Moral: Get a gun! We know one man who made quite a " bag' from the bedroom window—but that is another tale.

* - The result of the first prosecution under the Insurance Act will administer a shock to the complacency of those who believed that the courts would content themselves with a fine of one shilling in case of failure to pay contributions. Anybody who went forward in that delusion now knows that he is wrong. The fine of £5 in each case of failure imposed at the Lambeth Police Court should cause the " resisters " who have been deluded, to think again about their attitude. For this fine is in respect of one offence, and a new offence is created every week that there is a refusal to make the contributions. Moreover the employer does not escape liability even by paying the fines. He has still to pay the contributions, and in case of failure to do so he may be compelled to pay the whole of the benefits under the Act to any of his servants whom he has cut off from its direct help. Opponents of the Act would do well to find a cheaper kind of heroism.

The announcement that Mr. Hammerstein has done with opera in England recalls other " splendid failures" in this direction, and especially that of D'Oyly Carte. The latter after his long prosperity at the Savoy, and with the most popular of English composers at his back, built his opera house with high. hopes. But it was the very popularity or Sullivan which contributed to the failure. Carte, ignoring all precedent, neglected to provide himself with other composers and other operas. "Ivanhoe" was to make new history It ran a hundred nights or so, and there was nothing to follow. The house was closed, opened again for a while, and then was sold to Sir Augustus Harris's syndicate and became the Palace Music Hall. So, too, when the Covent Garden Opera began 1847 it became obvious that both it and tne rival at the Haymarket could not prosper. The latter, with the help of Jenny Lind' struggled on till 1852, when it was closed for four years,. only re-opening when the other

houPe was burned down.

A HISTORY OF WARRINGTON NONCONFORMITY. 'Forthcoming Articles by Mr. A. Mountield.

'This month Warrington Noncon- -sormity will reach its 250th anniversary. In recognition of this date, Mr. Arthur •,dourtheld has prepared an account of the 'early years of local Nonconformity,which Will appear in due course in our columns. The anniversary falls upon St. Bartholo- mew's Day, August 24th, and we have •arranged to publish the articles during the month of September. An account will be given of Robert Yates, the ejected Rector of Warrington, Who became the first Nonconformist minister. Many interesting documents Will be quoted and the history of local churches will be traced.

As no history of Warrington Noncon- formity has yet been attempted we think these articles will have more than ordinary ir.terest to a large number of Examiner" readers. The first article will appear on Satur- •day, August 31st.

EAST CARMARTHEN.

SCENES ON ARRIVAL. THE AIRMAN SENDS A WIRE. ma HUCKS READING HIS CORRESPONDENCE. Photos Burrell.

The long-promised visit of an airman to Warrington has now.* passed into local his- tory. On Tuesday afternoon Mr. B. C. Hucks, an English aviator who has already made a name for himself by creating some notable speed records, accomplished a remarkable flight, under trying con- ditions, from Southport to Warrington, alighted in Victoria Park, and re- ascended to give a display which thrilled and delighted the populace by its novelty and daring. This was the first occasion on which Warrington had been the objective of one of the intrepid band of con- querors of the air. True the towns- people had once or twice caught fleeting glimpses of airmen who sped over the town to take part in races between larger centres,but never before had the oppor- tunity of witnessing the alighting and ascent of an aviator been vouchsafed. It was somewhat unfortunate that Mr. Hucks's visit should have been robbed of a certain amount of interest by unavoidable postpone- ments. In the first place flights were arranged as a special attraction at the Old English Fair recently organized by the Million Penny Fund Committee but, in con- sequence of an accident to his machine, Mr. W. H. Ewen found it impossible to fulfil the engagement. Then came the announcement that on Saturday Mr. Hucks would fly over from Lytham, and throughout the afternoon large crowds scanned the skies more or less patiently in the hope of being rewarded by the advent of the bird-like machine of which they had heard so much. But it was all in vain, for at 5.30 a telegram arrived with the tidings that the miserable weather made flying out of the question. As was natural under the circumstances, people became sceptical as to whether the pro- gramme would ever be carried out, and the consequence was that comparatively few assembled in the vicinity of Victoria Park on Tuesday and many of those who did turn out seemed to regard their errand in the nature of a forlorn hope. The afternoon wore on without any sign of the airman. Then came a message that he had left Lytham and nothing had since been heard of him—a report which gave rise to all kinds of sinister suggestions. By 4.45 the crowd had dwindled down to not more than 100 and most of those were meditating the advisability of an immediate depar- ture when suddenly all eyes were rivetted by the appearance in the skies of a black speck which, growing bigger and bigger, soon resembled a huge bird. As it drew nearer, the whir of an engine was heard and excited cries of "He's coming" were raised, for the people knew that their weary waiting was at last repaid and that Mr. Hucks 'and his Bleriot monoplane—the Firefly "—were passing over their heads. The afternoon had been cloudy and threaten- ing but happily the airman's arrival synchronised with a welcome burst of sun- shine and the manceuvres of the machine, which was travelling at an almost incredible pace, were seen to perfection. Sailing grace- fully right over the park, the monoplane circled in the direction of Manor Lock, swept round to that portion of the park where the hangar had been erected, and came to earth with a smoothness which one would hardly have thought, possible—indeed there was not the slightest jolt. The machine ran beauti- fully along the ground for about twenty yards, abruptly stopped, ;and Mr. Hucks, beaming with satisfaction at the safe accom- plished of his trip, leaped out to receive the congratulations of the few who happened to be on the spot ta greet him. Mr. Hucks fully satisfies the mental picture which one usually conjures up of the successful aviator—an alert, calmly conident, resourceful young man, with plenty of sangfroid, but not the slightest semblance of braggadocio or" side," to whom flying seems the most natural thing in the world. Smilingly he posed for the photographers and then chatted freely to the Pressmen about his experiences in the air. Mr. Hucks said he left Lytham in the morning and flew across the channel to Southport, where he lunched with Mr. Gustav Hamel, another famous aviator who was originally billed to come to Warrington. Then the pair of them got out their machines and did some fancy flights for the benefit of the people of Southport. Later in the after- noon Mr. Hucks left for Warrington, Mr. Hamel keeping him company part of the way. The journey to Warrington occupied not more than seventeen minutes. " I was in the clouds practically all the way" added Mr. Hucks. "I had a frightful time getting here. When I went up at Southport the ,wind was blowing at the rate of 45 miles an

hour, without the slightest doubt. It actullly whirled the propeller round, against the compression of the engine—a most extra- ordinary thing. They had to hold down the machine while I made ready and then it only ran ten yards before it " scooted " up like a balloon. Hamel and I were both astonished at the way we climbed. We had never seen anything like it before. You can fly in a gale at the seaside without the slightest trouble—in fact the machine went as steadily as a bird— but the further you travel inland, the worse it becomes—it gets ' choppy ' and bumpy.' I have been troubled with wind only ten miles an hour in velocity, far more that I was with the tremendous gale to-day. I had some difficulty in finding the way here. I went over by St. Helens and twisted round a bit."

Mr. Hucks was not at all complimentary in his first impressions of Warrington—given quite spontaneously. "This is a very dirty place" he said. "You can see it is War- rington by the smoke." Mr. Hucks added that the highest altitude attained during the journey was 3,000 feet. He was unable to distinguish all the places he passed over. "These manufacturing towns" he remarked—" you cannot see down into them for smoke. When I got to St. Helens I could see my hangar here. It was just a tiny, speck of white. I knew I was getting to Warrington, because it was along- side the river ; that was what made me certain."

The news of Mr. Hucks's arrival had by this time caused many people to flock to the park and they were allowed to inspect the monoplane at close quarters. Next the machine was wheeled into the hangar, so that the mechanics might see that every- thing was in order for the exhibition flight later in the evening. There would be a crowd of about a thousand when, a few minutes before seven o'clock, the " Firefly " was hauled out of its temporary home. Owing to the difficulties of negotiating the trees in the park,Mr.Hucks decided to ascend from an adjoining field. It was getting dark and a mist was gathering when the eager multitude was roused to enthu- siastic cheers by the wonderful ease and steadiness with which the monoplane rose from the earth. Rapidly it wheeled round in Igreat circles and then made straight across the Cheshire. country until it was a mere speck standing out against the clouds. Higher and higher it climbed until it had reached an altitude of 3,500 feet. And then came the most thrilling part of the exhibition. The aviator shut off his engine and vol-planed with a series of corkscrew-like dives until he was once more within easy range of terra firma. The final surprise which Mr. Hucks devised for the delight of the crowd only made them marvel the more at his splendid control of the machine. Swooping down to the field from which he had started he almost touched the ground, and the spectators who had made certain that he was about to land, were apprehensive lest there should not be sufficient room for him to come to a standstill before reaching the fence which separated them from the field. Just at the critical moment, however, Mr. Hucks restarted his engine, soared over the heads of the startled crowd and, having described another eircle,landed with beautiful precision, to be received with the enthusi- astic acclaim of those who had been held almost spell-bound by his remarkable exhibition.

Speaking to the crowd through a mega- phone, Mr. Hucks told them that he had encountered a wind of from 40 to 50 miles velocity. " Flying at this place" he added " is very difficult ; you can hardly see it for smoke. Several times I lost the ground. I could not see it for smoke." Mr. Hucks also complained of the intense cold and, as the mist had thickened, he wisely decided not to attempt another flight. After his exhibition Mr. Hucks was enter- tained by Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall at Walton Hall. His machine was taken to pieces and sent by train to Leeds, where he gave exhibition flights on Wednesday. MR. HUCKS AT LEEDS. Mr. Hucks's visit to Lofthouse Park, Leeds, on Wednesday, aroused the keenest possible interest. At one time it seemed doubtful whether any flying would be attempted, not because of the wind —Mr. Hucks does not bother about that—but because a hitch in the railway arrangements left the aeroplane stranded between Warrington and Wakefield. Mr. Hucks, however, was determined to ful- fil his engagement, and with the aid of a

special train he got the machine to Lofthouse in the late afternoon. Once there no time was lost in fitting the planes, and by six o'clock Mr. Hucks was in the air. His first flight occupied twenty minutes, and during that time Mr. Hucks went right round Leeds, six miles away, came back to the rodrome, and then circled round Wakefield, three miles off.

I went up 4,500 ft." he told a pressman when he could escape from the cheering crowd. "It was rather awkward getting LIP, but at that height the wind was very steady. I was about 2,000 ft. high when I was over Leeds, but I could not see much of the town because of the smoke. At Wakefield I was over 4,000 ft. high, and I saw less of that than I did of Leeds." Al seven o'clock Mr. Hucks made another flight, but this time he stayed over the ro- dronie the whole time, executing intricate ma,nuvres. CONTRIBUTION TO THE MILLION PENNY FUND. The Million Penny Fund Committee wish to aqcnowledge the sum of D. 14s. received from the proprietors of the "Daily Mail," being a contribution from the gate money taken at Victoria Park on the occasion of the visit of Mr. B. C. Hucks. --- - THE CONQUEST OF THE AIR.

(By Alderman Bennett.) In dreams for years I saw my fellows flying, And now, at last. I see them fly in deed, In the blue air with the proud eigle vieing, Or with the burning sun-god's winged steed. With dauntless hearts they tempt the boundless ether The wonders of another world to win;

~,;;i:cii.ii;;;;;(de;ii;The-briiii-t-, breezy 'weather, Above earth's tumult and its toil and din. How often have I seen the swallow sailing, A thing of beauty, in the autumn skies ; Or watched the gull, o'er storm and sea prevailing, Right in the teeth of the fierce tempest rise ; Or seen the wizard bat, amid the gloaming, Wheeling, triumphant, round the silent farm; Or marked the laden bee, its harvest homing, . . . .

On gauze uplifted when the wind was warm ; And, noting how the clumsy peacock's pinions Could bear him to his perch amid the trees, And yearning to usurp their blue dominions, Have dareu to prophesy of da3s like these. What rapture to escape the exhalations Of the grim cities where red Mammon glowers; What new developments for all the nations; What endless vistas for men's growing powers! With slow, sad feet they learned the art of walking, A new delight alive in every limb, And set the prehistoric fogies talking By learning how to ride, to row, to swim. Then came the engine, through our green fields panting; And then the steamship, monarch of the seas ; The submarine, the depths of ocean haunting ; The big balloon, the sport of every breeze. The flying motor, still a thing of terror, A dragon snorting throueh our country lanes ; And now, the fruit of many an ancient error, The sky is dotted with our wroplanes. We shall be dowered with a strange new sensation; We shall be lifted to an ampler air. I hail the gentle art of aviation, But mix my salutations with a prayer. May this tremendous new achievement never Be used to further war's insane decrees; And soon may all the weary realms for ever - Be rivals only in the arts of peace ! ARTHUR BENNETT.

DUPED IN PARIS.

Warrington Artist's Singular Adventure.

A strange adventure has befallen a young artist named Paul Wotfgang Brunt, of John-street, Black- burn, who formerly lived in Warrington. Mr Brunt (says ths Paris correspondent of the Daily Chronicle ") arrived in Paris on August 14th, intending to spend three mouths in studying French art and the French language. On Saturday night, while walking along the Boulevard de la Chapelle, he made the acquaintance of some French-speaking people whom he was foolish enough.to accompany to an hotel in the neighbourhood. Mr Brunt remembers nothing more until on Sunday morning he found him- self wandering in a street at Vincennes, a couple of miles away. He had been drugged and robbed of his watch and about S.-20 in money. Presently he sat down on a seat, and his strange appearance attracted the attention of a policeman. As Mr Brunt was unable to give any clear account of himself the policeman took him to the station, but Mr 1 Brunt was unable to recollect his own name. 1 M. Pachot, the Commissary of Police, obtained the services of a young Englishman from the Vincennes Commercial School to act as interpreter, and by this means the English artist was able to tell his story to the police. As he was without resources M. de la Place, director of the Commercial Institute at Vincennes, kindly sheltered him and supplied him with food.

Mr Brunt does not know Paris at all, and cannot speak the French language, so M. de la Place sent his nephew with him to the British Consulate. After hearing his story the consul decided to repatriate him, and Mr Brunt, with a ve;y vivid recollection of his first visit to Paris, left by the night train from St. Lazar° for London.

ocal Option and Disinterested Management.

Sir,—l have been interested in reading the report of the Temperance Campaign Which was held at Bridge Foot. One of the speakers at these meetings was very definite in demanoing local option. There are many people who believe ii local option most sincerely but yet fear that the policy would not be carried in many industrial areas, and they are there- fore desirous of some additional policy which can be applied to those places which refuse veto. In the policy of the Temperance Legislation League, this contingency has been provided for and alongside with the proposals for a time limit, local veto and control of clubs, the League urges for a system of dis- interested management and for the adequate pro- vision of counter-attractions to the public-house. it is important in view of the fact that Lic-rising Legislation is promised that certain definite opinions should be formed as to the basis of further legislation. Our legislators will have to take a wide outlo:k and formulate a bold and comp ehensive policy. It is to be hoped that the working of the company system in Scandinavia will be given due consideration, and that such a system adapted to our own country's need, will be given to those districts which refuse veto. It is certain that in the matter of Temperance Reform we have got to experiment further, and disinterested management is one of those experiments which it is imperative we should try. The growing volume of public opinion in favour of such a comprehensive policy augurs well for the future, and one hopes that when the Government take this matter in hand they will give due heed tJ this proposal.—Yours truly, HUBERT 'BEAUMONT.

The Guardians and Alcoholic Stimulants.

Sir,—ln your very interesting report of the proceed ings of the Warrington Board of Guardians last week, I note that it is stated that the amount expended in the hospital for alcoholic stimulents is probably over £l2O a year. If one might judge from the reported discussion there would seem to have been a preponder- ance of opinion that this is a strictly moderate and necessary expenditure and that the adverse criticise arose from a mere fad on the part of the Rev. A. M. Mitchell. I think it will be the experience of the board that the rever- end gentleman is well able to defend his statements. I venture to intervene to point out that there is a general tendency throughout the country towards reducing the amount spent in intoxicants in the treat- ment of disease. The majority of the medical pro- fession can certainly not be styled teetotal bigots, yet it is a notable fact that an increasing number of doctors connected with our great hospitals are depreciating the use of alcohol. Sir Victor Horsley has given some remarkable figures covering a period of 40 years, with regard to expenditure alcoholic liquids in seven of the leading London hospitals. The totals include expenditure on both staff and patients. In 1802 the beds occupied numbered 2,254 and the expenditure on ale lolic liquids was £7,712. Since that time the amount spent on alcoholic drinks has consistently decreased until in 1902 it was only ;C2.925 and the beds occupied were 2,309. It is further pointed out that although the beds occupied by patients show only a slight increase, yet the actual number of patients is much larger, because the recovery from operations is far more rapid than formerly and beds are vacated more quickly. In the Infirmary of the Wandsworth Union in the year 1875 with the patients numbering 1,405, the cost of wine and spirits was £371. In 1905 while the number of patients had increased to 5,451, the cost of wine and spirits had decreased to £2 7s. sd. There is a huge mass of evidence with further support of the contention that alcohol is of little or no use in the treatment of any disease. The Warring- ton Guardians would be well advised to inquire further into this matter in the interests both of patients and ratepayers —Yours, &c., THOS. D. LESLIE. (United Kingdom Alliance).

Sir—With reference to your report of the last meet- ing of the Warrington Board of Guardians it is surely time to make some public protest against the appar- ently systematic obstruction which the Rev. A. M. Mitchell meets with every time he tries to do his duty. Is it to be understood that he is the only one who will not shirk an unpleasant duty? If the amount spent upon alcoholic stimulents is moderate and reasonable in proportion to the number in hospital what is there to conceal? If it is not, the public have a right to know whether some of the guardians are indifferent•or not. It is not necessary to agree with everything the Rev. A. M. Mitchell says in order to realise that he is doing a great public service in opposing all kinds of extravagance and unnecessary secrecy. Let us hope he will not grow weary of his uphill fight.— Yours, &c., RATEPAYER.
