Paltry Progress.

Sir,—Whilst one must confess that L. 0. V. is correct in his criticism of party politics, I as one who believe in independent political action and industrial organization, am compelled to cross swords with him when he places the strike in the same category as Home Rule or the Grand National.

Whilst the former can be counted as necessary political reforms and the latter dismissed as of no importance, the strike must be recognised as a means of economic readjustment. After dismissing the strike as futile for the purpose of abolishing poverty, L. 0. V. says "The first duty of a nation is to see that the people have a .healthy animal existence." First of all, we must recognise that we are living under what is known as capitalism, and that the nation, the people, have not as yet undertaken the collective responsibility of providing the common necessaries of life. There exists between employer and employee a cash nexus only, and not the personal nexus of co-equals. One employs for profit ; the other works for wages. The profit of the employer determines his sJcial status, just as the wages of the worker deter- mine his condition of life. High profits spell ease and luxury, travel and amusement ; low wages spell physical degeneracy, slum life and blank despair. One is the antithesis of the other ; profits fail as wages rise, and vice-versa. That is the bed rock of present-day society and the prime cause of all social struggles. All are affected, none escape. It is as true to-day as when those pregnant words were uttered by Payne :—" Those who are not for us are against us." If poverty is to be abolished then capitalism must be fought, and no amount of sentimental fudge is going to accomplish it. The worker who enters into the employ of a firm agrees to sell his labour for so much money, which means so much bread, beer and clothes, or common necessaries of life. When increased prices and competition amongst workers for jobs, have reduced these conditions to a low level (and this is constantly going on) they come together and agree to demand increased pay, which means better conditions. This often necessitates forcible means of accomplishment, such as the strike. So then, we see that strikes are necessary to maintain eten a bare subsistence level. Organized Labour is in the position of an Army constantly at war, yet burdened with a majority of its soldiers who refuse to take up arms in their own defence. The very fact of the workers occasionally revolting against these wretched conditions, is the only proof we have of the inherent desire of men for economic freedom and should be sufficient inducement to all opponents of slavery to join with them in this struggle. L. O. V. says strikes for better wages will not abolish poverty. That might be true but a ten per cent advance in wages gets uts two shillings in the pound nearer to that happy state of being. My experience has been that life on 30s. per week is to be preferred to life on 20s, despite the fact that both con- ditions are poor. To tell us that the first duty of a nation is to see that we have a healthy animal existerace is putting the nation outside the people who compose that nation. Who are the nation, the few plutocrats and politizal tricksters, or we, the workers, who produce the life force of the nation? How are the workers going to get what they want? My answer is by industrial and political actions, not only to obtain an animal existence but to enjoy life as we the producers wish. 'nose who are content to receive the charitable offerings of their employers in the shape of cheap amusement and recreations instead of asserting their right(which means might) to partake of these things in return for their labours, not with servility but with dignity, are no more free than the

SMOKE.

THE HOUSE-WIFE'S RESPONSIBILITY.

[TO THE EDITOR OF THE " EXAIIINER.I Sir,—Allow me to supplement Alderman Bennett's admirable letter on smoke in your last issue, by referring to a particular aspect of the problem which he missed. He refers wholly to manufacturers' smoke under the control of men. I would like to refer to domestic smoke under the control of women especially to the prevalent custom of firing chimneys by contrivances appropriately known as Demons.' Anyone who has been partially suffocated by the acrid and pungent fumes from a chimney on fire will appreciate the seriousness of the nuisance.

..„. Owing to the lowness of the chimneys and the weight of the fumes—l know nothing of their chemical constitution except to infer that they must be highly poisonouT, com- pounds of carbon—these fumes pour down- wards into the surrounding streets and houses partially, suflocatiuti 'every living thing near. Nor are these fumes easily dis- persed. In the narrow streets they are pent up between the houses and hang about for a considerable time.

The excuses which are made for the excess of manufacturing smoke cannot be pleaded in this case, and one must only conclude that in the great majority of cases the air is deliberately polluted in order to avoid the services of the chimney sweep. In some towns the police successfully keep the nuisance in check.—Yours faithfully, J. MANSON. 8, Winmarleigh-street, Warrington. • PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Patrons of the Hippodrome are well satisfied and highly deligtited with this week's programme and as a. consequence this popular house is crowded nightly. Two better or more proportionately built athletes than Lievin and Pantzer it would be hard to find. When they first appear before the footlights the audience is spellbound with admiration, but when these clever entertainers have performed some of the most daring flying hand to hand balancing feats with real clever- ness and exceptional dexterity the approbation of the audience is unbounded. A neat, smart and up-to-date athletic performance. " Fine Feathers "is the title of a Lancashire comedy study in which Rousby and Rene excel themselves. With the homely Lancashire dialet the couple are fairly at home, and many amusing situations are witnessed and enjoyed. The sketch abounds in genuine clean humour and the audience is not slow to appreciate. Alburtus the First and Jessie Millar give us something of a novelty in "A scene at the fairground" in which is introduced juggling and comedy, and the lady also proves herself to be an able performer on the cornet. The couple are assisted by Dolly, who controls the fun of the fair. George French gives us many catchy and up-to-date songs in a breezy manner, and is most successful in his latest success " The day I scored that goal" in which he humourously depicts a "leather chaser" in his various positions and trials throughout a game of football. Ina Lorimer greatly pleases as a Scotch character comedienne, her songs being well-chosen , and her highland dancing excellent. Lillie Tiny and ' Nellie Mite are two clever juveniles, and as singers and expert exponents of the terpsichorean art, they are decided favourites with the audience. Billed as "The Dainty Little Star," Mona Warwick is indeed a clever entertainer for her age. Attired in chic and • becoming dresses she gives two songs in a very acceptable manner and thoroughly deserves her generous tiibute of praise. Walter Saxby is reallyA funny customer and possesses a unique style and ab abundant supply of witty gags, and above all two very . catchy songs. The usual attractive pictures are shown on the cinematograph. _NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. Atlas, the famous athlete, and Miss Vulcana the strongest lady living, who has created the new world's record in France and Spain, and who is described as the most beautiful, symmetrical and physically perfect woman on earth, together with the sisters Atlas, champion strong girls and little Gus, boy champion head a good bill for next week. They will be supported by Will Netta's Jockeys, in a novel racing act, Alfred Wellesley,supported by Miss Fanny Olive and Mr. Telly Dillson in a comedy " Uncle's, courtship " ; Damerell and Rutland, comedians Three Flying Winskills, somersault and pirouette performers ; Billy Curzon, the confidential dame . Rouschle, impersonator ; and the Two Brilliants, " champion girl Xylophonists.

" TORYISM AND TREASON." , QUESTIONS BY A LABOUR ORGAN. Under the heading " Toryibm and Treason," the "Labour Leader" this week asks :—" How is the Government going to act towards responsible political leaders who openly encourage outrage, sedition, and treason? Is the matter going to begin and end with examples of the polite letter•writer given by Mr Winston Churchill, Mr Bonar Law, and Sir Edward Carson? Do the Government mean husines.;, or are we merely being treated to a sham fight with blank cartridges and coffee and cigars for tare.t.? If Mr Churchill's statement of the case Lolds good, if his view is shared by the ther members of roe Cabinet, the question is surely one for the law cfficers of the Crown. .

"What do Mr Boner Latv's Ulster doctrines amount to? They either mean nothing, or they mean that he is prepared to support op-n rebellion and civil war against King and country. In our opinion he has distinctly encouraged Ulster to take up arms against the state in the eveLt tf Home Rule becoming law, and if there is to be no public prosecution we fail to see how auyon., cm be prevented in future from advocatmg bloodshed and violent revolution as I a means of thwarting a law which he may not happen to approve. . . . The Government must make up its mind whether it is going to tolerate treason when treason is uttered by the lips of rich and influential men. The issue is urgent, and will have far-teaching consequences." .

SHREWSBURY FLORAL FETE. _— S'arewsbury's thirtl-eighth srmual foral fete—the greatest show of its kind in the kingdom- took pla.e on Wednesday and Thursday and accied another chapter of brilliant achievement to the anualv ot the Shrop- shire Horticultural Society. As on previous occasions, the show was held on that ideal ground, "the Quarry," which, with its undulating greensw:rd and rich back- ground of foliage and river, is one of the most picturesque venues which could be imagined for such a function as a floral fete.

_ All told the entries totalled about 2,000 and were well up to the average of the few years preceding 1911. But wailst this is in itself a pleasant retlaction, it has to be remembered that the numerical phases of a show are not, after all, the most important ones. It is the quality of the extibits which confers tie reputation in the first degree. In this respect the current Shewsbury fete stands out conspicuously ahead of all its predecessors. Confirmation of this is found in the official report of the judges duly attested on the records of the society as follows :—" We think the exhibits generally are above the average and the taste in arranging is a decided advance on tiie previous year. The exhibits of fruit and vegetables are exceptionally good." This, in a couple of sentences, chrystallises the opinion of some of the most noted authorities in the horticultural world. The most famous growers, amateur and professional, in the country had entries. One of the most remark- victories of the day was that of Lord Harlech, who won outright the " Shrewsbury Chronicle " Cup, awarded to the gardener whose employer secures the greatest number of points in the aggregate of the com- petitions. Lord Harlech exhibited in between sixty and seventy classes, and Captain Heywood•Lonsdale - competed in about fifty classes. When the computation of points was made it was found that Lord Harlech (gardener, T Lambert) bad become the owner of the cup. A non-competitive exhibit which drew to itself con- stant coteries of admirers was a grand specimen of the sacred flower of the East—the lotus. This plant, despite a week's journey from Egypt, is in a good state of preservation, and charmed all beholders.

MILL GIRL OR SOCIETY

BELLE.

No matter in what station of life a woman may find herself she will still have mmh in common with all her sex. The shawl of the mill girl and the furs of the society belle cover a woman —no more and no less. Both are alike. Nature des not discriminate between them. She is no more kindly to the one than to the other. The same awful pain in the back or sides, the same dreadful sense of depression and feeling of general bearing down, the same utter hopelessness ef it all are the experience of both. For both alike there is a remedy, good enough for the rich woman and cheap enough for the poor. Women all over the world in all stations ot life have benefited by it. It is to be found in thousands (if happy homes in this country. It has dispersed the shadows and let the sunlight into many a despairing woman's life. It is the discovery of a woman. It is soothing, gentle, harmless, yet effective, and every woman should try it. Go into any chemist's and ask for Mrs., Lydia E. Pinkham's Vegetable Compound It will cost 2s. 9d. (or 4s. Od. for a much larger bottle). The Lady Manager. co The Lydia E. Pinkhain Medicine Co., 42a, Hampstead Road, London, N.W., will send it post paid at these prices, or she will send free a Private Book to women only. THE CYCLIST'S NOTE-BOOK.

[By " Silver Street."

Devonshire and its roads..----We had to stay at Appledore a day longer than we had planned, on account of having advised some friends to address theit letters to Launceston by a certain date. As it was, we had to leave the little town a good deal sooner than we would have desired.lnstead of taking the direct Clovelly road, from Bideford, which was sure to be crowded with market carts and traps, coming in to Bideford Market, for Tuesday was market day, we sought a less crowded way up the little Yeo Valley, a tributary of the Torridge. There we found the procession just as bad, pei haps aggravated by the narrow road. S 9 for four miles we were dodging this traffic. Worse still a violent downpour came in the middle of it, and made two thuds of the road useless for cycle's. The drivers were politeness itself ; in every case they draw into the soft mess ate the side and left us as much ef the crown as they could. For tbr-y have a special recipe for road repairs there. "To two tons of road metal add three tons of road scrap- ir,gs, sod parings, &c ; roll until the metal appears at the surface ; it is then ready for all kinds of traffic except modern vehicles." In a week or two, the road, say 24 feet wide is divided into three sections. he centre is fairly fit for riding on ; the other two-thirds is a mass of mud in wet weather, a depth of dust in dry, and wretched ruts in between wades. Devon- shire roads are the worst we have seen in all England or Wales. You can rely on a week in the Devonshire combes pulling down your average, but the awful state of the roads added to their slope will make your cyclo- meter ready to kick you out of the county. Before reaching Clovelly you ride three miles through the Hobby Drive and don't grudee the six- pence, for it is a beautiful ride. All motors and waggonettes are stopped at the entrance; only the "push bike" may be ridden, and small gigs 'or traps allowed through. Tne surface is excellent; the gradients easy, and the delightful shade of the trees is constant as the track winds around the sides of the ravines till it ends at the top of the staircase leading down to Clovelly harbour. It is no use taking the cycles down with you. As an Irishman might say, no one but donkeys can climb down to the sea or back again. But notwithstanding the crowds of trippers, and the notice boards announcing "Teas, lodging, or beds," Clovelly is beyond all words delightful, and on that sunny afternoon the flowers on all hands, yet dripping from the recent rain, made it appear Just like all Devonshire packed into one little bit of a sample. But there, Kingsley's description barely does it justice, so why throw away words on such a place. The place from which everyone should see Clovelly has been made accessible by the opening of the Hobby Drive. The view is unique, and that summer afternoon nothing could be more beautiful. The blue sea, the white cottages tumbling down to it, the grey harbour, the green forest, the purple cliffs beyond and Lundy Island lying far away—" see Clovelly and then When we reached the top of the moor above, the wind was for the first time at our hack, blowing North west. Before we reached Kilkharnpton on our way to Bude we had reason to bless the motor charabancs. Our "innards " were calling for tea, for the ups and downs of those Cornish moors (we were at least in Cornwall) break up the molecules of the last meal very quickly; a road-side farm announced " Good teas provided daily," and, inquiring, we were shown into the tea room. It was a sight that would have made a Sunday school tea-party's mouth's water, Stewed rasps and clotted cream "by the galore," six or eight different kinds of cake, flowers decorating every yard of table, teas Bet fer thirty or so, and all this miles from town or village. We asked the black and white young lady if we had intruded on some party's good fortune. "Oh no, we provide this every day," it was for the charabancs and coaches which always stop there on the road to Bude, Hartland, Clovelly, Bideford and other holiday centres, so we did justice to our " innnards " and were charged 9d. each. We then went on to bonny Bude, but we were badly diddled there at the Hotel.

I CLOSE TIME FOR WARRINGTON CYCLISTS Ever since I have ridden a bicycle, I have maintained that a cyclist ought to be excused from the custom of raising his hat to a lady. It is a pleasa...t act of politeness, but like many more things it is abused. We are not allowed to nod to a chum, if he happens to have a girl with him. Whether we know her or not we must raise cur cap to him. That is alright for pedestrians, but when you are negotiating a particularly nasty bit of grease on the cycle, or crossing Warrington Bridge, or plunging into any one of the many bottle-ueckeo alleys which we call our main streets—Eforsernarket, Buttermarket, Chester-road, you are sure to see Arthur coming along with his best girl, and a correspondent asks um should not we be excused from the dangerous practice of having to raise the cap while we steer with one hand only. Some of the newer sparks, I will admit, can ride just as well with both handles at liberty and sweep dovvn 80ld-street putting imaginary gloves on all the time. But for the old stagers, there should be a close time allowed. Therefore be it enacted by the general body of cyclists in parliament assembled that from the first day of September to the last day of August each year

no cap, hat, bowler, wide aw a're or silk shall be lifted, raised, moved or shifted by cyclists when meet- ing, overtaking or running down any lady, or her dog or other companion whether man, fellow or gentleman. Provided that during such time as the new Bridge at Warrington is in construction the aforesaid period shall be extended so as to include the whole year. Now, I think that will meet the rase. WESLEYAN CYCLING CLUB.

Last Saturday was a blank day for most cycle clubs. The Wezleyans, instead of running to Whitegate, followed the wind to Warburton and Dunham. At Bollington Mrs. Bragg provided us with tea, but the evening had to be spent in waiting for the rain to cease. It did not however, and we had to seize the opportunity of a slight lull to run the 8 miles home. It proved to be the best hour of the evening Number present 14. DEATH OF GENERAL BOOTH

THE GREATEST APOSTLE OF THE AGE.

With deep regret we announce the death of General Booth. The veteran founder and head of the Salvation Army passed away late on Tuesday night at the age of 83. For some days grave bulletins have been issued, and it was known that the end could not be long delayed. After a period of unconsciousness he passed away at 10.13 pm. Without regaining Consciousness.

There were present in the death chamber at the time Mr. Bramwell Booth and Mrs. Bramwell Booth, with their eldest son and daughter, Commissioner Mrs. Booth Rehberg, youngest daughter of the General, Dr. Milne, Commissioner Howard, and Colonel Kitching. The nineteenth century knew no greater soan than General Booth. He took religion into the slums ; he carried his crusade throughout the woad, journeying thousands of miles every year, even when over 80 years old. The world, indeed, has lost the greatest apostle and missionary evangebit of the ags.

THE NEW GENERAL.

It was officially announced on Wednesday niuht that Mr. Bramwell Booth. eldest son of the late General, and Chief of Staff for the last 32 years, had succeeded, on his father's nomination, to the command of the Salvation Army. The nomination was revealed on the solemn opening i of a sealed packet at the Army headquarere, n the presence of the solicitors and the commissioners now In London; The envelope had teen sealed exactly 22 years ago on Wednesday.

A MEMORABLE VISIT TO

WARRINGTON.

A Fupplement to the " War (7ry issoed yesterday contains a special article on the late General's visit to Warrington, from which we extract the following : Historic importance attaches to the Sunday Campaign which the General conducted in the Hippodreme at Warrington, on April 28th—it was the last of his dearly-prized opportunities fur coming into cl,)se grips with the people, to whom his heart was ever drawn out.

During a flying visit which a " War Cry " repre- sentative has just p lid to the town, he was fortunate in meeting c%ith Mr. Thomas Hewitt, a prominent builaer and contractor, and a staunch friend of the Army, who was the prime mover in securing the General's presence. "I had it laid on my heart," he AA , " that the town of Warrington were in need of a visit from the General and I prayed that he might come. One night I wen down to the Hall and said to the Adjutant, " Do you think it might ba pos.-ible to get the General to Warrington before he goes to Heaven ? advised me to see Commissioner Lavtley, which I did, with the result that an appointment was made for a week- end Campaign, but he was unable to keep this. (This was one of the very few occasions on which the General did not manage, by hook or by crook, to fulfil his engagements.) Still, I tried again. The General agreed and everything was going swimmingly when be met with an accident at his home, and the townspeople were bitterly disappointed. Then one day I got a note from Commissioner Lawley : " Lock up! The General's c ming !" And he came.

ONE OF THE CONVERTS. "You are highly favoured to have me," said the General as I welcomed him, 'for it is my last cam- paign before I go into dock for repairs." "I have heard," Mr. Hewitt continued 'from above one or two, among them the top people in this town, of the good impresion he made. There was a woman who seemed beyond hslp because of her drunken habits ; I saw Ler kneel on the stage beside her own father, and the change in her is a positive miracle. She is a msinber of one of the Churches in this town now. The General has left a deep mark for eternity on the place." "To think that mine was the last theatre for the General to appear in ! " said Mr Arthur Turpin, the manag.r of the Hippodrome, whs was cesply stirred at the thought. " Two thinga among many impressed me on that day," this gentleman c mtinued. " One, the great surprise it was to me in the afternoon that a man of his age should be able to stand there and speak to that crowd for one hour ! I "clocked "him, so I know. And, two, that he should•follow this with an hour's sermon in the evening. I've never been a critic of the General, and I was glad of it that ISunday afternoon, fr he put " paid " to lots of !accounts that day."

COMEDIAN FORGOT Ens " BUSINESS." Then Mr. Turpin told us a story. A comedian sought me out on the Sunday night to discuss his " lines" for the next week's show, and he found me listening t) The General's sermon. I made a place for him to sit at my side, but we forgot the "business," and both just followed that address. Occassionally my companion would cough and wipe his eye, and at the close, following a moment of utter silence, he gasped, " Love us ! What a top of the bill! What a top of the bill ! " That exclamation we may add, is about the last word of admiration in theatrical par- lance.

Mr Frank Macnagliten,the general manager of the circuit of theatres of which the Warrington Hipp- drome is one, has always shown a warm interest in the work of the Army and a deep, personal regard fo; the General, which he showed by placing his theatres at his dispoE•al free of charge, and sparii,g no effort to make his meetings successful. "Do all you can,' be wired to Mr Turpin on this occasion, 'to make the General comfGrtable."

The finest sight of the day was to see the General among the penitents who Came and knelt on the stage around him. The first five were men, among whom were more than one of the special "guests" of the evening who were there, at the. invitation of the General, which had reached them in the public-house bars through Brigadier Aspinall or Major Cater on the Saturday evening.

A. LESSON IN SCULLING.

BY RICHARD MARSH, Author of "The Beetle," "The House of Mystery," ",Tom Ossington's Ghost," &.43.

[Miss Whitby writes to her mother.]

My Dearest Mamma,—l have had the most delightful time you can poasibly think of. Everybody and everything has been so nice! And Charlie has been teaching me sculling. And —6ll, what do you think?—he drowned me! Yee—completely! Only, of course, it was all toy fault. And he pulled me out of the water by the hair of my head—or something; I don't know what, or: how. Wasn't it noble of him? I never enjoyed anything so much in my life. But I will tell you all about it. I know you must feel anxious. Only don't think I'm dead. because I'm not. I haven't even caught a cold. All owing to Charlie. He says I wasn't in the water long enough; that's what he says: I assure you I was in the water quite long enough for me! _ _ _

You know, •ever since we've been down here we've been on the river every day, Charlie and I His mother—Mrs. Mason, you know—doesn't care for the water; she says it's damp. But I think that's because she knows that two are company, and ,is tender-hearted—like you, my dearest Nlomkins! Besides, she likes fussing about, and paying visits, and she is good—l hope that I shall be as good as she is one of these fine days! But you can never tell! Of course, it was very nice being pulled about —by the ways did you over hear the story Charlie told me? Some man said to a silly woman: "Mrs. Jones, do you mind my pulling your daughter about on the river?" She nue from her chair "Do I mind your pulling my daughter about on the river? You'll be asking roe next if I mind you pulling her about in my own drawing-room! I don't know what the young men of the present day are coming to!-" And the acquaintance was dropped. Only Char- was so aggravating! He wouldn't do in the least bit what you told him. I would say to him before we started:

-I'-ditailie,-.Wo--ia,ke me for a long row—now,

promieo me!" And he would say: "Certainly. Fourteen miles out, and fifteen in.,,

"Don't be silly I I wish you would—l do so like to be pulled" He would be standing on the bank, with his back to the water, and with me just in front of him. Ho would stretch out his arm.

"Tip us your flipper!" He meant: "Give me your hand., ' When he chooses, Charlie can be slangy. "I'll pull you into the river." It was not the slightest, use my 'talking. I would sigh, and get into the boat, and hope for the best. But I never got it. No! As soon as we had gone three or four hundred yards Charlie would pull towards a little island, which is just beyond the bend in the river—l don't know who put it there; I know that I often wished that it was further—and row right round it into a sort of little creek which was on the other side, which was just large enough to hold the boat, and where no one could see us be- cause of the trees. So far as privacy was con- cerned, we might as well have been in the heart of a virgin forest. And there Charlie would stop, and do nothing else but talk. Though I'm bound to confess that he chose interesting sub- jects of conversation as a rule, because generally. when he wasn't talking of himself, he was talk- ing of me. And it is such a help to conversation when one is well acquainted with the topic under discussion. But he did so annoy me, because he would never do what I told him. I wanted him to row me to Oxford or somewhere. But he said it was so hot—l didn't feel hot!—and Ox- ford was twenty miles away, and more. That was nonsense, because quite little electric launches go there and back in • a day. At least, I am nearly sure they do. But what irritated me more than anything else was because he kept on asking me why, if I was so fond of rowing, with the thermometer four hundred degrees above bursting point—l don't believe it was anything like so hot as that, but that is what he said—l didn't row myself. He knew I couldn't. But I made up my mind that I would learn, and that, what is more, I would teach myself. I would show him what I could do. So one morning 1 got up all alone, quite early, without breathing a single word to anyone. I don't know how early it was, but I know it was early, because, when I had let myself through the dining-room window—French win. dow—into the garden, there was not a creature anywhere in eight. The garden runs right down to the river. The boat is kept tied to,the bank. I pulled it close, and got into it—and directly I got into it it wobbled. Dearest mamma, even at that last moment—or at that first moment, whichever it was—l almost wished I hadn't come. Suppose I should upset! I do believe I should have gone straight back again to bed, only I couldn't. The boat had drifted to the end of the string, and was ever so far from the land, and how to get it back again I didn't know. So I sat still, and scarcely uared to breathe. But it did seem so silly to sit still like that. If anybody saw me, what should I .ay? I had a pair of nail scissors in my pocket, and with them I cut the string. They were a very small pai, and the string was thick, and it was wet. It took me a lone time to cut it. But I succeeded at last. I was adrift on the waters!

. --...—.....- Dearest mamma, have you ever felt what it is like to be adrift, all alone by yourself, in a dingliy?—you know what that is, am sure. I think that is how it is spelt. I hope you never have, for your own sake. It is awful! I could have screamed, only I dared not, for fear of up- setting the boat. I had never thought of the oars until I was adrift. And when I did think of them my heart went into my mouth—be- tween ourselves. I believe it was there already. They were generally taken out of the boat at night. But, fortunately, Charlie had been too lazy the evening before, and had left them in. And there they were, staring me in the face. I took hold of one, very gently, but directly I be- gan to lift it the boat began to wobble. I tried to think I didn't care. I clenched my teeth. And I kept on lifting the oar. And at last I got it straight up in the air—like a scaffold-pole. I had had no idea it was so heavy. It was all. I could do to hold it. In fact, I couldn't hold it. 'l%ft my horror, it slipped out of my grasp, and fell into the stream with a splash. It. drenched tlle with water from head to foot. And there it was, floating about by itself, ever so far away. I quite abandoned hope. I gave myself up for ;est. I tried to collect my presence of mind, .and th Lo think of the Royal Humane Society's ree- ons for drowning—which are printed on the board in Hyde Park, you know. Judged by the light of after events, losing that oar was the most fortunate thing which could have happened to me. If I had not lost it, I should have drowned myself. My body might have been ly- ing at the bottom of the river even now. But I did not know that at the time. And, after I had abandoned hope, it was all I could do to keep from crying. Suddenly someone called to me from the bank. It was Charlie. He was not very well dressed. He had his towel over his arm. He was going for his morning bathe. But I don't think I ever had loved him so much as when I heard his voice and saw him Standing there—no, not even in that glad moment when first he told me that he loved me, and asked me to be his wife.

drowning!" "That's all right!" It sounded unfeeling. But I knew what he meant. I'll swim out to you." Ho leaped head foremost into the river, as if it had been nothing at all, and swam out to me, as if ho had been a dog. lie swam, first of all, to the oar, and then he swam to the boat. "Sit still!" he said. But it was not the slightest use my sitting still, when he himself nearly pulled the boat right over. Almost before I knew it, he was sit- ting on the seat in front of me, sopping wet, and laughing._

What's the meaning of this?" he asked. "I'm learning to row." " You looked as if you were learning to row! Well, have you learnt?" "Charlie, you're not to laugh at me! It isn't right. Some girls have people to teach them rowing—people who care for them, that is. But I haven't. So, of course. I have to teach myself. And I have to get up in the small hours of the morning to do it. too." I sighed—or I chose to let him think I did. "I might have been drowned."

"That's trite—vou might." He looked at me hard. And I befieve there was a twinkle in his eyes. But as I looked right past him, far across the water, and he saw that I was serious. I think that it went no further. "took here, Miss

"Very well, I won't. Look here, young person— " And you're not to call me - young person either. I'm not a young person." He went on: "I don't lcnow if you're awaye that it's easier to learn rowing with one Scull than with two?"

"How was I to know that? No one ever told me. Nobody takes sufficient interest in me to tell me enything." "If you had betrayed the slightest sign of desiritis the information I wreuki Lave taker,

"Do as I do." 7 I did as he did, or I tried to. He put one of the oars in its place in the water without the slightest difficulty. I did not find it by anY means so easy. .* May I ask, before we proceed any further...l4 it is your intention to knock me overboard?,, He said that simply because I happened to Int him with my oar as I was lifting it. The thing would not go right. I daresay that I did knock him two or three times, but there was really 1?0 necessity why he should make a fuss, as he did do. "Is your scull having a row with you or are you having a row with it? What is the matter?" "Thank you; nothing is the matter." I scorned to complain. "I'm glad to learn it; I hate to bear of people falling out. Now are you all right?" "I am perfectly right." He glanced round to inspect me. "Yes, you look perfectly right. You've gel your scull the wrong way round." I turned the thing. "Now you've got it upside down." "Whatever do you mean? You don't mean to tell me that the other end ought. to be in the water." No, I don't mean to tell you 'quite that, but I do mean to tell you that you ought to hold it so that the hollow part of the blade looks in front , of you. It's an elementary fact, but it is a fact.' I turned the thing again. "Suppose you put three or four feet more of it out of the boat. .A 8 you're holding it at present a good part of your scull seems to run to handle." I pushed some more of the thing through the place they call the rowlock. "I didn't tell you to put the wholo of it out of the boat; it's just as well to keel" something to catch hold of, if only for the look of the thing. If you observe, there's a strip of leather round the scull. That strip of leather marks the point where the scull is sup- posed to rest in the rowlock. That's better. Your hands are wrong; shift them. Hold your scull as I am holding mine." It was all very well of him to talk like that, but it was most unfair, besides being ridiculous. His hands are at least twice as large as mine; he could get right hold of his ear, while I could scarcely get hold of mine at all. But I feclinecl to argue. "Now, when I say 'pull,' pull. And it's about time that somebody did begin to pull. or very shortly we shall be aground. Now, pull!" For some reason, I don't know what, the boat began to turn right round. Charlie immediatelY stopped rowing. I had never begun. Of course, at onoe, Charlie tried to be funny. "I see the progress of ,his boat is going to be,, conducted on the tee-to-turn principle. May A ask why you didn't pull?" "Because I couldn't." "Why couldn't you?" "Because I couldn't get my oar out of 04 water." "So I should imagine. There appears to be six feet more of it in the water than-there ouglit to be. This is not intended to be a lesson 1 punting. In punting one desires to feel the bed of the river; you and I do not want to get quite SO doep." "I wish you wouldn't laugh at me." "My dear :\lay, zoti,;•-.: can be further frore my thoughts. How could I dare? Let us trY again. There's no hurry, take your. time; there's no fear of our go' ing ashore just yet. At present we look more like crossing the river. :gow, are you readYl When I say the word—pull!" Again the boat began to turn. "Charlie, I cannot get my oar out of the water. I'm not as strong as a horse." He looked at me and laughed. I could have /aughed, only I was afraid of crying; it was so rexing to feel one was so stupid. "When I was a small boy, and I first started to row, I couldn't get. my oar out of the water, except when I didn't want to, and then it came Ut too easily. THE ABBEY OF YALE ROYAL.

The old Abbey of Vale Royal has been brought very much under notice during recent months. Mr. J. H. Cooke, of Winsford, has published a finely-produced historical novel which has its scene at Vale Royal, and which seeks to restore, in imagination at least, the glories of the departed stones. The newspapers of the past week have told us that the tenant of the Hall, Mr. Dempstor, has pierced the soil and after locating the Abbey walls has exposed them to view after long centuries of concealment. The old place deserves a resurrection. It was one of the finest abbeys in the land but razed to the ground in the reckless movement under which Henry VIII. destroyed the religious houses. Had it lingered to its decay like the old piles at Kirkstall, Melrose, or Fountains it must have been a monument of beauty and the chief antiquarian glory of Cheshire. .

We have told in this column the story of the Abbey, but in view of recent happenings a few further notes may be timely. The story of the foundation of Vale Royal as told and believed through the centuries, ascribes it to a pious vow of Prince Edward, afterwards King Edward I. Returning from the Holy Land he was in peril of shipwreck and promised that if spared to reach England he would found an Abbey for a hundred Cistercian Monks, whereupon the vessel is said to have righted herself and been brought to port. The legend has an artistic finish. No sooner had the Prince landed and the last seaman disembarked than the vessel divided and every fragment vanished under the waters. The distress of Edward is depicted in a quaint old painted window which adorns the present hall of Vale Royal. Wind, wave and the devil all try to pull down the ship, which is miraculously held aloft by the Virgin and carried to safety in spite of all the hostile powers. Edward was heir to the throne when the promise was made, but did not take any steps to fulfil it. There came a day, however, when a house was founded and in 1277 the site was consecrated by Anianus, Bishop of St. Asaph. A GREAT DAY AT VALE ROYAL. On August 2nd, 1277, Edward Lt now king lof England, laid the first stone on the site of the high altar. It was a great day. The Earls of Gloucester, of Cornwall, of Surrey and of Warwick and the baron of Manchester laid stones in honour of Christ, the Virgin St. Nicholas and St. Nichasius.

_ Queen Eleanor laid one for herself and one for her son Alphonso, and the Bishops of Bath and Wells and St. Asaph celebrated High Mass, -

After fifty-three years of hard toil the I Abbey was consecrated, but not for another twenty years was it anything like complete. Few who saw the stone laying ceremony lived to see the consecration and probably not one saw the fine pile complete. King Edward was generous towards the Abbey. The treasury accounts show that £32,000 was granted towards the building,

and this is an immense sum when allowanoe Is made for the value of money and the cheapness of labour in those days. The Abbots did not enjoy smooth and uneventful years. Quarrels with the tenants over whom they had been placed led to blood- shed and the peaceful vale witnessed regret- table strife and open warfare. _ _ _ _ _

That there should be friction is not a sur- prising thing for the Abbots had unusual powers over the tenantry. The baliff. was the judge in criminal affairs and could -com- mand their services for warfare. The Abbots had a prison and their jurisdiction was powerful. It is significant, too, that a place called "Gibbet Hill" is in the vicinity. The head of the Monastery ruled like a minature king over central Cheshire and would need unusual discretion to control the rough and not over-contented sons of the soil. THE DISSOLUTION OF THE MONASTERIES.

Some pathetic documents are preserved which relate to the period of the destruction of the Abbey. Henry VIII. in his scheme of wholesale plunder sent commissioners to report upon the Abbot and his men. Two noble letters survive in which Abbot John pleads for his Abbey. He travels to London, as quickly as illness will allow, to ask that the blow be averted. Needless to say his journey does not avail. The commissioners among other things report that the " Vieker of Weram " (Weaverham) hid said that "the Kynges Grace was not lawfully married : " That was enough. Someone in the neigh- bourhood had spoken against the King's marriage and the wreckers appeared' on the scene.

The ruin of the house was quickly accom- plished. Sir Thomas Holcroft of Croft bought the land and the ruins for £450 10s. 6d. _ . _ _ .

For three centuries the Abbots had reigned in Mid-Cheshire like little kings and main- tained a style equal to the most powerful ' barons. By one stroke the Abbey was des- troyed and its inmates left homeless and scattered. So complete was the destruction of the building that it has been surmised that it was made a condition of the sale to Thomas Holcroft that he should utterly destroy it. Why its massive walls were not left like those that still adorn our country is one of the mysteries. Only tradition has kept alive the memory of a stately building but there has been nothing what- ever to show whether the stories were true.

A TENACIOUS TRADITION.

That tradition does sometimes show itself surprisingly accurate is well known. Sometimes in deed it helps the archxologist. forgive gdi drag a instance v g such in an ioe n degression) an old man told an antiquary that a certain mound in the parish was haunted by the ghost of a soldier who wore golden armour. The heap of turf did not appear to promise discoveries but upon opening it ths diggers found that it was a burial place, and along with some very faint human remains was a fine polished breastplate of brass. The warrior who wore that shining armour had been buried in Anglo Saxon Times. There was no pro- bability that the grave had been touched. For thirteen hundred years at least the story of that golden armour had been handed on. This is wonderful; it is well nigh incredible. And now all the old traditions of Vale Royal are showing themselves to be well founded. Its huge pillars are exposed and its proportions are being gauged. It was on

CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB.

A BIRD STORY. A,LINNET'S NEST IN ST. PETER'S SQUARE.

The Manchester "City News " tells the following incident : St. Peter's Square, Manchester, was on Saturday last the scene of a romance in bird- life. During the summer months, as our readers are well aware, it has been the prac- tice of the Parks Committee for some years to brighten the dulness of the city by placing in the small open spaces still left to us shrubs from the Corporation's nurseries at Carring- ton Moss. The polluted atmosphere, how- ever, quickly damages the shrubs, and from time to time those which languish are taken back to the nurseries and replaced by fresh shrubs. On Saturday morning one of the new arrivals in St. Peter's Square was a large privet bush. It had not been there long before some boys were observed poking sticks in the top of the bush. Their behaviour attracted the attention of a policeman, who found that it was a nest which had aroused the curiosity of the boys, and that the nest contained your young green linnets. The policeman, having first taken the kindly precaution of getting a brother officer to guard the nest, went and reported the matter at the Town Hall. Alderman Harrop, chairman of the Parks Committee, promptly arranged for the shrub and its precious burden to be carted back to the nursery. Here, we are glad to state, the parent birds soon found their lost offspring, gave them a rapturous welcome, and attended to their needs.

HE MUST DIG.

He wanted a job, and, like every cne else, lie wanted a good one, you know ; Where his clothes would not soil and his bands would

Iseep clean, And the salary mustn't be low. He asked for a 10 a, but they gave him a spade, And he half turned away with a shrug, But he altered his mind, and, seizing the spade He—dug ! He woiked with a will that is bcund to succeed, And the months and the years went along. The way it was rough and the labor was hard, But his heart he kept filled with a song. Some jeered him and sneered at the task, but he plugged Just as hard as he ever could plug; Their words never seemed to disturb him a bit As he dug. The day came at last when they called for the spade, And gave him a pen in its place, The joy of achievement waR sweet to his taste, And victory shone in his face. We can't always get what we hope for at first— Success cuts many queer jigs; But one thing is sure—a man wil' succeed— If he digs.

But- let not the visitor form large expecta- tions. Masses of long-buried and discoloured stonework are all that will reward his pilgrimage. Only a student of Ecclesiastical architecture can be expected to go into raptures over thc m.

Yet the visit may be very interesting for all that. From Whitegate,so called from the great Gate House of the Monastery of the White Monks, there is the lovely avenue of beeches to admire. This leads to the secluded mansion of Vale Royal which stands upon part of the site of the Abbey. One important spot can be identified. There is a mound locally but foolishly called " The Nun's Grave." This is the site of the high altar. The stone rock remains underground, but a ring of majestic elm trees surrounds it and to uncover the altar base would mean considerable trouble and the destruction of the trees.

It was upon this spot six and a quarter centries ago that King Edward I. and a brilliant company laid memorial stones and gave their blessing to the enterprise. The fair open pastures would spread around him as they surround us to-day and by his express wish the lovely spot was called "Vale Royal." Savo for the Hall he would see the open Vale as we see it, and it is hard to realise that since that August day in 1277 a mighty Abbey has risen, flourished, and perished, its very site covered for centuries by the emerald grass and golden buttercups. And it was the greatest Abbey ever built on British soil. Yes, the old place deserves a resurrection. But what a pity they did not leave tho.se glorious walls I A.M.

'raking several weeks at random, it was ascertained that last year an average of 622 massages—and some of them very long ones—had been received weekly. CANON DROWNED. Falls Into Swollen River and Is Swept Away.

- The Rev Charles Vincent Gorton, hon. canon of Manchester Cathedral, who bas been living at Hereford, fell into the River Wye on Tuesday evening and was drowned. Mr Gorton, who was partially paralyeed, was staying in the district for the benefit of his health. The river flows vast the bottom of his garden. The tody has not been recovered owing to the swollen state of the river.

SOLDIER-ASSAILANT KILLED BY BIS OFFICERS. WARSAW, AUG. 20. . _

An extraordinary affiir occurred this morning at Powonski, an outlying suburb of Warsaw, where some troops of the engineers' corps are in camp. A private, who had been sentenced to arrest, entered the flat of Prince Nicolsi Tumanoff. the commander of the battalion, and began to One bullet struck the Prince in the lungs, another shattered the Princess's leg, and a third wounded an lire his !Hauser carbine. orderly. in the abdomen. Hearing the shots, some officers rushed into the flat and killed the assailant with their revolvers.—Renter.

GAS WORKERS' RECORD STRIKE PAY. During the part quarter, ending June 20, the National Union of Gas Workers and General Labourers paid out £9,003 5s 9,1c1 (in 16,901 payments), an increase of £3,279 Os 61. The amount of dispute pay was the largest in any quarter since the establishment of the union, except, perhaps, at the period of the South Metropolitan gasworkers' strike of 1889-90. In addition 1,000 guineas was granted by the executive for the relief of members out of work through the coal strike

NEWSPAPER OWNER'S PUBLIC BEQUESTS. Owing to the recent death of his widow, the public, bequests of the late Mr J E Taylor, of the "Manchester Guardian,' have become payable. These are £20,000 to the Victoria University, Manchester, £5,000 to the London Temperance Hospital, £I,OOO to the Manchester Infirmary, end £l,l-00 to the Manchester Grammar School. Mr Taylor was very generous in his gifts of works of art to public institutions during his lifetime. To the British Museum he gave, among other things, a complete set of Turner's " Liber Studiorum "; to the Manchester Whitworth Institute ho gave more than 103 water-colour drawings, including a number of Turners; and he was largely instrumental in obtaining for the Victoria Albert Musuem the finest Persian rugs which it possesses.

WOMAN WHO GAMBLED AWAY £40,000. When Lilian Burlinson, an elderly woman of Putney, was charged at the South Western Court on Tuesday, with drunkenness, a eoctor, giving evidence,said that at one time she kept a stud of horses, and that she had gambled away a fortune of £40,000. She was ordered to find a surety, or go to prison for a month.

The Examiner, Saturday, August 24, 1912.

Are Your Kidneys Weak? HOW WEAK KIDNEYS CAUSE BACKACHE, GRAVEL, URINARY DISORDERS, DROPSY, LUMBAGO AND RHEUMATISM. WARRINGTON CASE

Every moment of your life, poisonous waste keeps getting into the blood from your food and from the wear and tear of the body. But the kidneys filter it out and keep the blood pare. Every twenty-four hours they remove about 500 grains of urinous poisons and three pints of waste water from the blood.

But when the kidneys are weak, they cannot do their great work thoroughly. Then some of the poisonous waste gets left in the blood, and spreads disease to all parts of the system. You begin to feel dull, heavy and drowsy •, your back is bad, your head bad; dropsical swellings appear in the ankles or under the eyes, and the heart becomes affected. You may get rheumatic - twinges, lumbago attacks, or dizzy spells. The bladder acts too often, or there is pain in seeking relief. There may be sediment, gravel, or cloudi- ness of the water.

Whrn the kidneys are ill they need a kidney medicine—ordinary medicines won't do—they cannot cleanse and relieve the kidneys. Doan's Backache Kidney Pills act quickly and directly upon the kidneys and bladder —help the kidneys to filter the mischievous kidney poisons out of the blood and dispse of them through the urinary system. Doan's Pills are certified pure, and give the best results of any kidney medicine tnowit.

WOMEN'S GHAT.

Luggage Risks.—During the summer holidays, that nimble person known to us es the luggage thief has resumed operations. The other day, a valuable bag belonging to a German Princess was stolen in broad daylight at one of the busiest of the London termini, but from one case of the kind that gets into the newspapers, there are hundreds which are Dever heard of outside the immediate circle concerned.—Usually well- dressed, possessed of unbolted assurance, and sometimes carrying a cheap bag, the luggage thief finds careless travellers at railway stations a ready prey.— Usually the victim is a woman. People put down their bags on a seat or elsewhere, and are then ccncerned about getting their ticket, buying a paper, or saying good-bye to their friends, and in a few moments find that their belongings have disappeared If caught in the act, the thief is profuse in bis apologies, for his mistake in picking up the wrong blg.—There are nanny variations in the devices adopted.—Sometimes the bag carried by the thief is a mere skeleton, so designed that it can be set down over the article, or case, to be stolen which is then picked up by spring teeth, and carried off out of sight.—lt is an old saying that "if there were no gate, then there would be no sharps," but with the absence of system of registration, and the haphazard way in which our luggage is left about and dealt with at the station, it is a wonder that more things are not stolen horn us, during railway journeys. Vi7oreen's Virile Habits. We are told that man means to reassert his superiority over women, by growing side whiskers, as was the fashion in early Victorian times.— Apparently he thinks it the only resource left tobine,and he is getting tired of being told that woman is overtaking him in virile babits.—The last straw was added when a French professor declared recently that women were no longer the weaker, but the stronger sex.—That might be true in some constitional aspects, but not even tee most ardent militant suffragist would claim that woman is the equal of man in muscular strength. She Is hardy and strenuous, as is chew!' by the light- hearted way she takes life in all weathers. One usually finds that it is the man, and not tha woman who makes the most furs over rain-spoilt excursion', which have been the lot of so many holiday makers this so-called Bummer.—The average woman is never afraid to venture forth on a wet day, and what is more, she has learnt to dress for such conditions. In a plain but becoming outfit, with a hat that will stand all weatbers, the girl of to-day is often the brightest figure, if not the only enthusiast, who can be found to enjoy herself under lowering shies and persistent downpours. Coloured Umbrellas.—Hitherto the attempts to intro- duce the coloured umbrella have not been very successful, but possibly owing to the wet summer, there is a decided tendency to adopt this reform. Dark shades of purple and navy blue are most favoured and they go well with cloth or serge suits of corres- ponding colours. Makers are naturally rejoicing in the ict that the wet summer has helped to wear out millions of umbrellas, and it is said that Mr. William Sangster's book "Umbrellas and their history," has been in demand for holiday reading. In Europe, the umbrella was preceded by the parasol, which, in France, was carried by ladies of fashion in the days of Louis XIV. Joust Hanway, who died in 1786, was the first man who regularly carried an umbrella in London. He is mentioned by Dr. Smiles in "Self help," and praised for his moral courage as " the first man who ventured to walk the streets of London with an umbrella over his bead." There is in the London Museum, an umbrella which George IV. used to carry. It is of green silk, fringed with gold, and is about twice the size of the umbrella of today. Alpaca and steel ribs were first manufactured about the middle of last century, and it was not until then that umbrellas began to come into

general use. bhort skirts and Motor Veils.—Two features that strike observant onlookers at the seaside are the extreme shortness of skirts, and the popularity of the motor veil. For years, the seaside girl has eschewed hats altogether, but this season she is apparently pinning her faith to the motor veil, draped more or less—it must be admitted generally less—artistically about her head. The effect usually leaves much to be desired and beyond the fact that the veil tends to keep the hair tidy, there is nothing to be said in favour of the mode. It does nothing to protect the face from the sun or wind, and the result at the end of the holiday season will be a crop of wrinkles to deplore. Sea-faring Raiment.—All the marine shades of blue come to the front as £oo n as the yachting selson begins, and this year is no exception to the rule. The sea- faring raiment prepared for this autumn is simplicity itself, its charm consisting in the perfection of cut and the grace of line. Sponge cloth and serge are the favourite materials, and nothing could exceed in smartness the suits of two materials composed of blue serge and thick shantung. Tailored suits of this description have in many instances collars of miniver, which is to be used very much this autumn. This fur is really ermine, the black spots inserted at regular distances, being the tips only of the tails. oiaiti New 6leeves and Panels.—The suit of two materials has probably led to the introduction of sleeves of a different fabric from the dress. With Berg., sleeves are of stiff silk, or brocade, and with these the collar band generally accords. This is an excellent idea for using up odds and ends of materials for which a use may not be found. Tight sleeves have rows cf buttons down the outside from wrist to elbow. Panels are much used on the new blouses, all are narrow at the neck, and widen at the waist. Frequently three are used, and much embroidery is worn on them. Basques appear on many of the newest gowns, even when these fasten down the front. Silk and Cotton.—The trimming of cotton fabrics with silk, is not a new idea, for it came into fashion some years ago, and has never quite gone out. This year, the notion has been extensively to the fore, and undonbtedly it gives a cote of distinction to a simple frock. Muslin is particularly suitable for trimming with silk. Velvet is another decoration that goes well with cotton, and moro especially linen snits. A blue linen suit, trimmed with white velvet bands, intended for Cowes, was especially smart. Both silk and velvet trimmings are detachable, and easily removed when the dresses they adorn are sent to the laundry. For many ceremonial functions that took place late in the season, inexpensive material—such as muslin, or mercerised lawn—were used with excellent results for the dresses of young girls, and were far more suitable to the wearers than rich silk, rr stiff brocades. Sleeping with Open Windows.—Much has been and is said, concerning the necessity for sleeping with ones windows open, but enthusiasts often appear to forget that in our cities open windows frequently spell addi- tional suffering from the incessant no'se of buzzing, whirring traffic, and that in the country, in autumn and winter, at any rate, the damp night sir, is often better excluded unless one can have a fire in the room. Then in quite small bedrooms, where the window opens on, or nearly on the bed, difficulties in the matter of fresh night air frequently present themselves, but whenever possible, sleeping with open windows is undoubtedly desirable, and the bed pushed into the farthest corner, with a French curtain arrangement, should meet the requirements of all nervous beginners, and sufferers from chills and neuralgia. Cider and Gout.—Sufferers from gout have long been accustomed to regard cider as an enemy of the deepest dye, but a leading medical paper holds different views. Gout, it says, is rare In the cider countries, and, pro- vided it is made and kept under satisfactory conditions, authorities on the subject are declaring that cider not only protects against gout, but diminishes and suppresses attacks.

FOUND. THE GLAD EYE— and it was Mother's. —.

A " TOLL " BAR. One wintry night,between thirty and forty years ago I found myself stranded in the village of Luddenden Foot, in the West Riding. J was told that a 'bus would go my way an hour or so later, and I took shelter within the public-house from which the con- veyance was to start. As I waited, the local constable entered. Be walked up to the bar, wherein sat the landlord. The latter rose, toll a tumbler, drew into it a quantity of raw spirits, handed it to the fellow in blue, who tossed off the contents, turned on his heel and left the premises without a word having been uttered on either side. Evidently there was a perfect understanding between the two, and the call for a cheap drink was a regular occurrence. I have observed the same sort of thing in Oldham since then ; but there the offending constable, running greater risk of detention, was much more careful to see that the coast was clear before slipping inside for his nip of whisky. In one instance at Brighouse, well within my recollection, an inspector caught one of his men in the very act now in question, and the publican had to pay £5 and costs for supplying with liquor a constable on duty. The latter was obliged to give evidence against his supposed friend, and was, of course, himself suitably punished by his superiors.

1-WIIY SUBMIT? - Once, at Coroholme (which has latterly suffered under a prolonged strike of bobbin workers), finding a local publican serving a uniformed policeman with drink, I told him of the Brighouse case, and advised him for his own sake to drop the practice. He admitted that it was getting "beyond a joke," adding that the constable whom I had just seen called so often and was so persistent in his demands that to satisfy him cost " well into the teens of shillings a week." I told mine host plainly that I considered him a great fool to submit to it. He retorted that he could not possibly help himself, as to offend the " bobby " would mean some kind of trumped-up case ere long. I advised him to communicate with the local superintendent, assuring him that the constable would be promptly dealt with, hut be was clearly sceptical, or afraid of being himself seriously involved. A GLANCE AT THE OTHER SIDE. To be sure even in the rather bad old days to which I now refer, there were police constables who were incorruptible and undefiled. On one memorable occasi in I attended an athletic meeting in a district where betting had been "winked at" as now alleged in the case of Widnes, When the bell rang for the opening event, the " bookies" got to work as had been their wont, and the police on duty thereabouts at the moment took no notice of the illegality. Presently, however, a young constable who was there for the first time, happened to pass that way, and, taking in the situation at a glance, proceeded to warn both the bookmakers and the officials that if the betting did not immediately cease, their names would be taken and the offences would be reported. His fellow-constables really disapproved of this, but of course they dare not openly resent it, indeed, they had no alternative but to make a pretence of backing him up. I knew another conscientious and ambitious officer who in like manner made himself singular by reporting two of the principal hotels in a Yorkshire town for offences against their licences ; and was afterwards so persecuted by fellow-constables who had habitually " nursed " the places against which con- victions had resulted that he ultimately became Gemented and had to be lodged in an asylum. His malady took an extraordinary form of monomania Some of his jealous and revengeful comrades repeatedly told him that, if he had worn different boots from those which he affected, he would long before have been promoted to the rank of sergeant. When this had been going on for some time he began to stop me in the street and ask if I saw anything amiss with his footgear. At first, I answered as a matter of simply courtesy, but presently I perceived that that poor fellow's brain was affected. " Nothing the matter with 'em, isn't there? "he asked,

'JEWELLERY FOR NOTHING." The Warrington County Court case in which it is alleged that local tradesmen were victimised brought to my mind some cthers of which I read years ago, and it may be useful to reproduce the facts here. A particularly strange case was that of a woman, who was a frequent visitor at jewellers' shops, and who was generally accompanied by a young girl. Fre- quently after their visits to a shop, something would be missed, but though they were suspected, were even arrested and searched, nothing was ever found on them. It was through a quarrel that the police ulti- mately rolved the mystery,when it was discovered that the girl's throat was of such a peculiar formation that she could conceal rings and other small articles of jewellery therein and could keep them there for a considerable time. This was almost equalled by a case that recurred at Clerkenwell Sessions, Two prisoners were accused of "lifting " three rings from the c ,unter of a jeweller's shop. Two were found on them, but the third had disappeared. However,at the trial, being threatened with severe punishment, if they did not give up the third ring, one of the men pointed to his confederate and said " We've been in custody now eight weeks, and all the time this man has had the ring underneath his tor gue." And it was there right enough. Beneath the man's tongue was a somewhat large cavity in which, so his fellow-prisoner said, he concealed not only a diamond ring, but a number of sovereigns and a rolled-up bank-note. There's another story to the effect that one morning a carriage and pair drove up to the entrance of a jeweller's in Bond- street. A liveried footman let down the steps of the equipage, while a handsome and elegantly attired lady alighted. Tho proprietor of the establishment in question hurried out and escorted the prospective customer into the interior, where she requested to be shown some articles of jewellery suitable for presenta- tion. A tempting array of trinkets was accordingly displayed for her selection; but after examining them one by one, evidently with keen admiration and delight, she finally decided to return later in the day, and make her choice with the assistance of a friend. Meanwhile, the carriage and pair had been slowly driving up and down the street, and were now some little distance off. The courteous jeweller, therefore, went out and hailed the coachman, who immediately drove up to the doorway. The lady was escorted back ' to her seat, the coachman cracked his whip, and the carriage and its beautiful occupant were driven rapidly away. They were never seen again. Neither were a handsome diamond brooch, a tiara of brilliants and a costly pearl necklace, which the " lady " had adroitly abstracted from the.counter while the jeweller was hailing her carriage ! GRAVE AFFAIRS. It first occurs to me that, although I was formerly guilty of a bit of doggerel now and then, I have per- petrated little of the kind for months past. Perhaps, therefore, I need not apologise for tipping a stave thusly : "Eh ? An' what 'bout yes'day's funeral ? Why, then, 'Liza-deary me 'Twas out-an'-out the shabbiest this 'ere village e'r did see. - There was th'family an' three more ; That was all—not 'art a score; An' if oven less in number we'd ha' gone short at the tea! Yu remember my pore 'usbands--Ebenezer, Joe an' Sam? An' y, to clam. know, 'cos you was bidden, ave my guests didn't As for little Thomas Arthur, An' our Benjamin an' Martha— Why, I buried every one of 'em wi' cakes an' beef an' mem ! " There are two other little stories that I wish to narrate in like manner but "ye cannot bear them now." With editorial sanction they will be con- tinued in !' our next."

JOURNALIST.

TIIE POLICE.

The quotation from the "Police Review, '.' ralieting on the Widnes constabulary reminded me of several incidents in my ovcn experience which I might hero set forth in order but for a feeling that I may have mentioned Borne of them in this column previously. Anyhow, I will venture to touch upon two or three of them, if ally in support of my sugge3tion that, not- withstanding the Widnes allegations, there is nowadays nothing like the whole.ale corruption of the police force, or collusion between them and the publicans, or blackmailing of the latter by the former, as obtained wben I was a young reporter.

-ROYAL MARINES' STORY IN PAGEANT.

The glorious records and traditions of the Royal Marines during nearly two centuries and a half have probably never been so strikingly brought before the public, as was the case in the grand pageant which was presented at the Deal depot by the Royal Marine Light Infantry. The Royal Marines have over 400 sea fights to their credit, a record of which the corps is very proud.

The pageant represented, in twenty scenes, some of the principal actions in which the corps has been engaged, starting with the operations against the Dutch in 1665. Our photograph shows the Pikemen and Musketeers of the Marines giving a display of drill as practised in the seventeenth century.

HATTON SHOW.

"Hattoo ,F-how is second to none of its kind " were the words of Mrs. Henry Roberts, of Hill Cliff, who opened the eighteenth annual show of the Hatton Horticultural Society on Saturday,and on looking round the tents at the various exhibits, one was bound to agree with her. The Rev. Hon. C. F. Cross (Vicar of Stretton) seconded Mrs. Roberts' eulogy, and spoke of the growth of the show from "a very small child " to one of much larger proportions. He would have those who were parents in that tent remember that they had plants of much greater value under their charge than those exhibited on the tables, namely, the children 'whom he hoped they would train and cultivate so that they would become a credit to the great nation in which they lived. Mr. Wright, of Daresbury, suitably responded. Mr. T. Litchfield, of Appleton, made a very racy and humourous speech on behalf of the committee and judges who, he said had done their duty very well indeed, especially the judges, for on looking round the tables he found that he had won several prizes, so he felt justifitd in saying they had done their duty. (Laughter and applause) The exhibits were on the whole a marked advance on any shown in previous seasons. The showery weather seemed to have kept the vegetables free from fly and other pests that affect 'them so much in a hot dry seasn. The onions were without question the finest ever staged. The celery also was capital. The cut blooms were very fine indeed. Some excellent heads of wheat and oats were shown. All that was wanted to make the show the best ever held at Hatton was fine weather but unfortunately was denied. Messrs. Plinston, Warrington, had an excellent arrangement of side shows which were fairly well patronized, despite the wet weather. The Walton Brass Band, under the leadership of Mr. W. Gornall, rendered very creditable music during the evening and Mr. W. Bannister desetvcs special mention for. a euphonium solo which he rendered with pleasing effect. A goodly number of young people engaged .in dancing until dark when the proceedings closed with the National Anthem. The attractiveness of the Show was greatly enhanced by several exhibits not for competition. Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart and Mr. T. H. Lyon sent magnifioient groups of plants; Mr. Robson J. Chorley had a fine display of roses; and Mrs. Massey of Acton Grange, showed excellent fruit. THE JUDGES. Messrs Thomas Antwiss (Whitley) and Edwin Britch (Appleton) acted as the judges in the farmers' produce section; Messrs N Nicholls (Ashton) and J Cook (High Legh) in the classes for oottagers' gardens and produce, whilst the adjudicators for the table decorations wore Mrs William Fletcher and Mrs P. J Ciorlev. The poultry judge was Mr Will Hooley, Birkdale. The other judges were : Wild flowers, Mrs Seoker and Miss Lee-Jones ; butter and eggs, Miss Whitlow, Appleton ; honey, Mr 11 Potts, Dutton.

Padgate. B9W LING MATClL—Beautiful weather favoured the return match between the representatives of Newton- le-Willows and the members of the Padgate Tennis and Bowling Club, played on the ground of the latter on Thursday. Great enthusiasm prevailed, and it was barely possible to finish in daylight. Both victors and vanquished expressed themselves as having greatly enjoyed the struggle for supremacy.—As the away match played previously at Newton resulted in a victory of 114 points for the Newton team, they claim on the two matches an advantage of 64 points.

200 116 Great Sankey._

ST. 'ATARI'S CHO-I-F-t -A—N7I) TEACHER; TRIP—The annual trip of the choirs and teachers of the St. Mary's Church, Sankey, and Penketh Mission, took place on Saturday last Ibe arrangements had been made by Mr F T Newbold (vicar's warden) and Mr E Dumbill (people's warden.) The venue was S.outhport, the ever-popular seaside resort. The party, accompanied by the Vicar, the Rev J R Jones, left bankey by the 105 train in the morning, and Southport was reached in good time. The party then spent the time in sight-seeing, and dinner which was served at Fletcher's Cate, was very much enjoyed. After dinner, the visitors sought the numerous attractions, which Southport is noted for amongst which was the tl ' exhibition by Mr. Gustav Hamel. Towards night they took train again for Sankey, all having had a very enjoyable day's outing, cuusidering the inclement weather.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

MAGISTERIAL DELAY AT WIDNES..

Much—ditr-.C-ulty was experienced on Monday in getting a masistrate to take the cases at Widnes. A de ay of over an hour was caused. Major Wareing explained that the gentleman who usually took his seat bad gone away from home, and he much regretted the delay of the court, which was due to having to send for another magistrate.

===== 0002601_19120824_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
POLICE SUPERINTENDENT HONOURED.,

At- Chester -Castle, yesterday week, Superintendent George Ennion, head of the Runcorn police, was decorated witn the King's Coronation Medal by Colonel France Hayhurst, chairman of the Standing Joict Committee.

Superintendent Ennion has had thirty-one years' service in the force, having joined on the 27th April, 1881. He has been stationed at Hyde, Crewe, Altrincham, Seacombe, Ellesmere Port, &c. He is one of four brothers who have served in the force, and one of these, Mr. Thomas Ennion, is the sup.rintendent of the Macclesfield police. Superintendent Ennion ivent to Runcorn in 1901, succeeding Superintendent Pearson.

DRINK IN A RUNCORN HOME. At Runcorn, on Monday, a young couple named Arthur and Sarah White, 6, Cross-street,were summoned for neglecting their five children. The evidence showed that the woman was of very drunken habits, and sold furniture and bedding to get drink. The children were very much neglected. The husband's defence was that after having been in prison for neglecting his children, be signed the pledge and got a good home together. After a year and ten months the wife became worse than usual, with the result that he broke the pledge. Be had done all he possibly could to get his wife to give up the drink.—The Chairman said there did not appear to be any evidence against the men, and he would be discharged. The woman would have to so to gaol for three months in the second division.—White promised the Bench he would get a home together.
