THE "RUINED" SKIRT.

DYERS SUED BY " OLD-FASID °Nib "LADY. At Buocorn County Court, on Tuesday, Ilenrietta Rushton, Wicksten-drive, sued Messrs. Wright and Co., cleaners and dyers, for £2 2s. the value of a skirt alleged to have been ruined in the dyeing process. Plaintiff said the skirt cost four guineas when new. and she had only worn it at three whist drives (laughter.) The young lady at the shop said it would " take black lovely " (laughter.) Miss Steele, assistant at Messrs. Wright's shop, said the skirt was slightly damaged when brought to the shop. She noticed it the dav after it was left. Plaintiff (holding up the skirt).—lf I went anywhere in that I should have to come home in the twilight (laughter.) Would 30U like to go to a whist drive in that? Witness.—No (laughter.) That skirt was fashionable six years ago. Plaintiff.—l'm an old-fashioned sort (laughter.) I have things to my liking. The defence was that the skirt was composed of adulterated silk. Judgment was given for plaintiff for El. CRUSHED BY STEAM ROLLER. TERRIBLE DEATH OF A WIDNES LAD. A fourteen-5 ear-old lad,named George Albert Davies, of 17, Farnworth-street, Widnes, met with a terrible death near Rainhill, on Tuesday evening. Ile was returning from work along Jubitts-lane, Rainhill, when a steam roller, belonging to the Whiston Rural Council was at work. Davies was noticed by the man in charge to be walking dangerously near the scarifier which was connected with the roller for the purpose of scoring the roads, and was warned to keep at a safe distance. The warning, however, was too late, for the unfortunate boy's foot appears to have slipped. He was dragged tinder the wheel of the roller before the engine could be stopped. The wheel bad crushed the lower portion of the body in a terrible fashion. A few seconds before tee occurrence the deceased's elder brother bad passed the spot on his way to work, and on hearing his brother's cries rushed back and picked him up. The victim, however, passed away in his arms. The deceased's rather was cne of the five men who were killed in the explosion at Muspratt'a Chemical Works, Widnes, some four mouths ago. INSURANCE INSPECTOR'S METHODS. OBJECTION BY RUNCORN JUDGE. His Honour Judge Stanger gave judgment at Runcorn County Court on Tuesday in the case of Kyeld and Kydd v. Miller. Margaret Niller, formerly an employe at the respondents' jam works, sued them in respect of an accident to her in February. She was operating a marmalade slicing machine, which stuck. She pressed on a rod with the object of starting the machine, which went back into gear, dragging her arm ‘vith it, and causing such injuries that the limb had to be amputated. She alleged that the engineer of the works had told her to press the rod. Respondents denied the latter statement, and contended that the accident did not arise out a and in the course of employment. Ills honour referred to the way a statement had been got from the girl by an inspector of the General Acci- dent Fire and Life Insurance Corporation, Limited. This gentleman, he said, went to Cheater Infirmary and saw the applicant in the ward, when she was, no doubt, in a poor state of health and very much depressed in spirits. There was no law that prevented the repre- sentative of an insurance company from getting a state- ment from an injured person. What he objected to was that the inspector should go at such a time, and when the woman was ill and weak, without saying he was coming and without the girl having a friend or adviser present. The inspector was at the hospital for two hours, and forced what was really a cross-examination upon applicant. Before he had put a single question to her he ought to have made it perfectly clear to the girl that she was under no compulsion whatever to answer and that he was only there on sufferance. Again, the person who was being interrogated ought to he allowed to give the statement in her own way and her own words. The matter was by no means on all fours with the taking of depositions before a magis- trates'clerk, who had legal experience an insurance company's inspector did not possess, and was also an absolutely independent person. His Honour found for the respondents, but would not allow costs having regard to the action of the inspector of the insurance company.

ST. HELENS.

IDEALS FOR RAILWAYMEN.

TRADE UNIONIST RALLY AT ST HELENS.

A procession and demonstration, organised by the St Helens Junction and central branches of the Amalga- mated Society of Railway Servants took place on Sunday afternoon, at Sutton and St Helens. The members of the branches, which are some 600 strong, met at the headquarters, Sutton, and walked in pro- cession, headed oy a band, to the Bridge-street Picture- drome, St, Helens, where a meeting was held. The Chairman (Councillor Forshaw) said he trusted that one of the immediate measures which the trade unions at.d labour forces were going to bring before Parliament on its resumption was an amendment of the Insurance Act, so that it would include a full, adequate, and generous maintenance for widows and orphans; and, secondly, provide that the coutributory basis should be swept away, and that, so far as the workers were concerned, the insurance should be absolutely non-contributory. (Hear, hear.) If the workers were ever going to get anything worth getting they would have to get it themselves, and the best start a man could make to that end was to get inside his trade union. Ali a matter of fact, no honest worker could really and truly afford to be outside his union. They should contend for something more than merely sending representatives to meet railway and snipping directors and colliery proprietors; they should send representives in sufliciest numbers to govern both the House of Commons and the local governing bodies. It was absolute folly for the workers to fight these men with strikes and then go to the ballot-box and vote for them for Parliament and local Councils, because they could and did use the machinery of government in order to secure the workers' defeat. When there was a strike Liberalism and Toryisrn were not worth a toss up ; and if asked to describe oilinial Liberalism and Toryism, so far as it concerned the real interests of working men, he should say that the Liberals belonged to the "better dead" school of politics, and the Tories to the " better never born " school. (Laughter). They should sink their old-time party politics and unite in common to sweep away the two orthodox parties and replace them with a party from the ranks of labour, under their own control, who would govern not in the interests of ground rents and dividends, but in those of the men and women who got their livings by their toil and brain. (Applause). Nlessrs. l‘PLean ano W. Jones (Liverpool), F Triplett (Birkeehead), and Kinsella (St. Helens) urged the claims of the orphan fund, and advised railwaymen to join the Amalgamated Society of Railway Servants, from mhich great benefits were received.

TERRITORIALS RETURN.

After what is generally regarded as the worst camp ever experienced, from a weather point of view, the Warrington Territorials returned home from Kirkham on Sunday. The main body of the battalion arrit ed at Bank Quay Station about 5 30 and had a hearty welcome from a large crowd. The men were looking remarkably fit and well and, despite the the torrential downpour of ths last fortnight,appeared to have benefitted by their training in the open. Lieutenant.-Colonel B Fairclough had under his com- mand 17 officers and 680 men of all other ranks. Having marched through the main streets to the Drill Hall, they were dismissed. About thirty men, under the command of Lieutenant and Quartermaster Ingram remained behind to assist in " striking" the camp. Men of the West Lancashire Division T. and S. Column of thelArmy Service Corps have been encamped at Kirkham along with the territorials. The Warring- ton Company have performed excellent work in camp, One of the local officers, Sergeant-Major Lawrence,met with an accident on Friday. He was leaving some of his colleagues, and as he turned fell over a tent-peg, sustaining a broken ankle. The writer of " Territorial Notes "in the "Liverpool Daily Post and Mercury " thus writes of "the secret of the unpopularity " of the Kirkham camp :—"You know, a fortnight or so ago there was an idea buzzing around, not only here in the old town but everywhere in and about West Lancashire, that Kirkham camp was going to be a sort of borne of rest for tired Terriers—a go-as-you-please Singlay school picnic, a kind of doles far niente, al fresco, take it easy, holiday—during which nobody would have anything to do, and everybody would make haste to do it, easily and with zest. The " boomers" of the spring of 1909 will shortly be "time-expired," and the notion was, so we were told, to let these chaps have such a truly lovely little outing that they'd just fairly jump like one man to sign on again for another four years at least. That was the idea, just precisely that and nothing more. Somehow, though, the picnic business didn't quite come off. As a matter of simple fact, it got such a swat on the head that it was crushed about as flat as a fly that vain- gloriously had tried to carry a steam roller on its back unaided. You see, the G.O.C. and the brigadiers, as also the gentlemen in command respec- tively of the artillery, the engineers, and the medical

THE EASTBOURNE TRAGEDY.

Eastbourne has been the scene this week of a tragedy involving the death of five per- sons. • A man named Robert Murray shot his wife and child, his wife's sister and her two children, of whom he was the father, and then took his own life.

After getting both families under one roof and killing his wife and children, he eu-

deavoured to obliterate all traces of his crimill by setting the house on fire with petrol. One of the women is likely to recover. Our photograph shows the house at East- bourne, where the tragedy occurred. The room with the windows removed is the one itt which the bodies were found.

LENIENT MAGISTRATES.

At Et. Helens, John Thompson, labourer, Russell- street, was charged on Monday with wounding his wife with intent to do her grievous bodily harm. The com- plainant stated that when her husband went home on Saturday nieht he had bad a lot of drink, and offered her two shillings as his wages. He used threatening language, and after some altercation, struck her on the head with a poker, having previously struck her several times in the face with his fist. So serious was her injury that she was found in the yard bleeding profusely from the bead. The prisoner, wben arrested, said he was sorry for what he had done. He did not know what he was doing at the time. He knew it was not manly to strike a woman with a poker. There were ten previous convictions against the prisoner, who was ordered to be bound over for twelve months in £5 and two sureties of 50s. each or in the alternative go to prison for one month.

£2O In Penalties.

The first summonses against an employer for failing to pay contributions under the Insurance Act were heard at Lambeth Police Court on Tuesday. when Mr. William Burlock, draper, of Walworth-road, was fined £'.s in each of three cases, with five guineas costs in the first, or £2O 53. in all. The magistrate also ordered the contributions to be paid. Mr. Hurlock stated on Wednesday night that he had been granted leave to appeal, and ;could fight to a finish.

THOUSANDS OF FISH POISONED. Wholesale poisocing of fish has occurred in the Cheshire canal near Sandbach and Middlewicb, and thousands have been found dead. Fishing matches have been cancelled.

units, bad quite another kind of idea in their heads. And I'm not so sure, either, but that their idea wasn't the better idea of the two. Anyhow, what they had in mind was work—real, good, hard, honest work— and not play at all. And why not? If the Terrier is to be a soldier, let him be a soldier, the real genuine article, and not a pinchbeck imitation that can't stand the acid test.

Ah. but, I hear you say, all work and no play makes Jack a dull boy. Ditto, Tommy. Well, I seem somewhere to have heard that sime saying before. Further, I'll grant right here and now that there's a great solid chunk of truth in it. But what would you? It wasn't any fault of the G.O.C. that, right throughout the fortnight, the clerk of the weather was in such a dolorous mood that scarcely ever for half a dozen con- secutive hours did he cease from weeping copiously. Mind you, it was neither the weather nor the work that made Kirkham camp a memory the boys won't ever allow their thoughts to dwell upon too lingeringly. The weather was vile ; but the weather is all in the day's work, and if you had seen the Terriers wallowing in the mud as I saw them, you'd say that they rather liked it wet than otherwise. Then the programme was stiff; but hard work never killed a man yet. I repeat that it was neither one nor other of these things that made the camp unpopular. No, the fact is that the country around and about Kirkham is terribly flat and unin- teresting. For beauty, and variety, and attractive- ness, it is not a patch on any one of the various places at which the division had previously pitched its tents. Then the picture of breezy Blackpool proved a delusion and a snare. The picture was there all right, but it wasn't 80 easy to get at as the boys had been led to believe. There you have the real secret of Kirkham camp's unpopularity. Too much was made before- hand of Blackpool's many attractions, and too little opportunity given to those attractions to attract."

WORKHOUSE CHAPLAIN'S GREEK.

Complaints were made at Tuesday's meeting of the Preston Board of Gul.rdiens that the entry in the chaplain's report book for Ribchester was half in English and half in Greek. The Clerk suggested that Sir Gearge Toulmin, M.P., should translate it, but 'lr. lq'azackerley urged that the Board ought to have it in English. Mr. Cartmell said that the reports ought to be kept in a tongue understood by the neople, and Mr. King remarlsed : "If I had my way I would send the book to the Local Government Board and ask them to deal with him and stop his nomen,e." It was decided to return the book to the chaplain.

ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKINGMEN'S ASSOCIATION.

(N.A. CENTRE).

The officials of the society wish to let members know that after last week end they are quite convinced that Norton Canal is alright and no more notice need be taken of the pollution scare unless they hear from them through the "Examiner." Last week end passed with- out a specimen of any kind being registered. Members who visited Chester, Minato Trafford, Hartford, Norton and Long Barn having poor returns and taking the angling all round it was a very poor week-end. We are now able to state the first prize for the annual match, held September 15th, at Newbridge: challenge cup, gold medal with the Warrington coat of arms enamelled in the centre, and a gentleman's dressing case complete. Value of the three .£1 Os. The committee are desirous of making this match the success of the season.

THE OrFICIAL'S 'MATCH. The ' president, vice-presinents, chairman, committee along with several members made the journey to Hart- ford by motor char-a-bane on Sueday last to fish their annual match. A start was made from headquarters about two o'clock and atter a halt at Stratton the journey was completed in good time. They arrived at Hartford stone fridge shortly after 3.30. While members were tackling up Messrs. Stout, Potter and Taylor bad the land pegged out ready for the contest. I It was at first intended to fish two hours, but owing to the inclemency of the weather the committee decided on an hour. We were soon at the fray but no sooner had the word " all in" been given than down came the rain and it never ceased during the contest. When time was called it was evident that those who had killed fish would be amongst the prizes which proved correct :- 1, W Bailey, 30z Bdrms ; 2, A Bradley, 3oz • 3, J Bryers, loz Bdrms ; 4, E Hill, loz arras. Winner of the Northern Anglers Centre match, J Dunbobbin with 30z 7drms. Out of 21 angling five caught fish. All the sport was spoiled by the downpour, and no sooner were we packed up and ready for the return journey than the rain abated. A start was made for Hartford about 7 p.m. After a halt at Acton Bridge we made for home, and arrived at headquarters at 9.15. In spite of the weather every- body enjoyed the outing. Tee 21 officials who fished the match had five prizes to compete for. This is the largest muster of officials that has been brought together for several years, and speaks well for the association's welfare in the future.

INDEPENDENT ORDER OF RECHABITES.

Warrington District No. 4.

The quarterly meeting of the above District was held in the lndeptndent Methodist schoolroom, Lowton Common, on Saturday, at the invitation of the new Tent (Salem) recently opened in the above schools. The meeting was called to order about 3 p.m. by the Chief Ruler (Bro It Barker), supported by the whole of the District officers and 24 representatives, also a large number of visitors being present. The minutes of the last meeting having been read and confirmed the District Executive's report was presented by Bro T Winstantley,and showed that a record number of meetings had been held during the past quarter owing to making the necessary arrangements for the working of the National Health Insurance Act amongst the branches. Five new tents had been opened (Salem Lowton) 95 members, (Good Samaritan) 44 members, (Zion) 39 members, (Volunteer) 22 members female branch),Rechab,ll members. The efforts of the Executive in endeavouring to obtain a large accession to the ranks of the District, had been most encouraging,the result so far showing that 1,230 new members had joined the various tests in the district, 645 men and 585 women, making a total State membership of 2,347 members; the membership on the voluntary side being 1,549 which was considered satisfactory. It was decided to hold the next meeting of the District in Thewlis street schools under the auspices of the new tent (Good Samaritan) on Saturday October 26th. The Juvenile Superintendents report was given by Bro J It Barker which showed that much good work, and continued progress was being made in the juvenile branches the total membership being 1,434. Bro T Massey, J.D.S presented a report of the pre- liminary arrangements for the carrying out of the coming of age celebrations of the juvenile district com- mencing on Saturday September 28th by a demonstra- tion and public meeting at Ashton-in-Makerffield to be followed on the Sunday by. quite a large number of Nonconformists pulpits being occupied by the Board of Directors and district officers, and a week of pub lbc Imeetings being held in various parts of the district. On Saturday, October 2nd, a Conference of superin- tendents with representatives from Lancashire and Cheshire districts will he held in Bold-street Wesleyan schoolroom at 3.0 p.m., to be followed by a puolic

meeting at 7.0 p.m., presided over by Mrs. James Dodds. Tea was provided by the friends iu the schoolroom.

A vote of thanks was moved by Bro Edward Walkden and seconded by Bro. B T Parkes and both speakers expressed their appreciation on behalf of the district for the admirable arrangement mide for the tea and meeting _ .

Bro Peter Gerard replied on behalf of Salem tent,and said he WMB both pleased and instructed with the work of the districts and he hoped that it would not he long before the districts paid another visit to Lowton.

TORIES AND THE INSURANCE ACT.

The Warrington Liberal Association is continuing its campaign in the various wards of the town, and on Wednesday evening a meeting was held at the corner of Lord-street, Latchford. It was prssided over by Mr. Eli James, who was supported by Mr. John Mather (secretary of the ward) and Councillor Iliddletcn, of Oldham. This particular part of the town is con- sidered a hot-bed of Toryism, and at the commence- ment of the meeting some of the women became very hostile, jeering at the speaker and throw- ing clay and mud. However, Councillor Middleton, was in no way daunted, and with that commendable persistency and zeal stuck to his task. Although at times he was inaudible he eventually succeeded in winning over the large audience to his side. The men who were present were most attentive from the beginning and at the conclusion confessed they were ashamed of the conduct of the women, who however, after listening to an eloquent oration on the benefits of the Insurance Act, gave the speaker their hearty support. The Councillor made the Insurance Act his chief topic and he dealt with his subject in a. masterly manner, explaining at length the various sections of the Act and emphasising the maternity and sanatoria provisions. To illustrate how thoroughly the audience appreciated the Act three hearty cheers for Lloyd George were unanimously given at the close. Another meeting will no doubt be held in the ward in the near future.

===== 0002601_19120824_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
SELECTED SETTLERS FOR READY-MADE FARMS IN CANADA.

All the provinces of Eastern Canada are making special efforts to induce emigrants desiring to follow agricultural pursuits to settle within their respective borders and the plan adopted by Nova Scotia is typical of what is being done to secure that end. Power has been obtained by legislation for the purpose of pro- viding assistance to settlers to take up farms who are not otherwise in possession of sufficient means to do saw If the individual to be assisted is found to possess adequate farming experience and is judged otherwise suitable, assistance, if need be, is given him to the extent of SO per cent, of the valuation of the farm he proposes to purchase, This guarantee, the Government arranges through a loan company. If, therefore, a man possesses at least 20 per cent, of the capital required and sufficient to start on his farm, after paying his pass- age, he is at once placed in a position of acquiring an independence under the most favourable conditions. Married men with families of this class who can com- mand from £lOO to £3OO capital, have here the chance of a lifetime, and the authorities in Nova Scotia, New Brunswick and Ontario, are prepared to receive applica- tions from any number of such people through their representatives in London who will furnish the fullest. particulars and advice on the matter.

However, ;the quality lett little to be desired, the first award itr.the cheese section going to Mr W J Lee, of Thornton-le- Moors, while Mrs Joseph Taylor. of Hatton, showed the best butter.

THE :JUMPING CONTEST.

To the majority of visitors to the show, the jumping contest comes easily first in interest, for there is some- thing quite exhilarating and exciting in the spectacle of beautifully-handled horses dashing round the enclosure and clearing gates, fences end pools with cleanness and comparative ease. Thursday's competi- tion was ore of the best seen at Waltou and the attractive, tree-bordered enclosure, commanding a pleasant prospect of Cheshire country, was surrounded by a large crowd which followed every phase of the event with the keenest possible interest. Twenty-one horses had been entered but about half a dozen of these failed to put in an appearance. However, it may safely be said that the cream of the entrants did take part, and the competitors a ere so closely matched that it was a difficult matter to decide who should be allowed to enter the second round. The first prize went to Messrs T and H Ward's bay gelding, Fisherman, which won at Walton last year and has a brilliant record of successes in the show world.Result:—First prize,( £2O) Messrs T and H Word, Pinchinthorpe, Great Ayton ; 2nd (£l5), Mr F M Grange, Alvaston, Nantwich ; 3rd (£10), Mr H Beeby, Melton Mowbray ; 4th (f 5 Mr F V Grange.

===== 0002601_19120824_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADESMEN'S EXHIBITS.

An interesting section of the show was that devoted to the stands and exhibits of leading firms who dis- played specimens of the latest agricultural implements, seeds, produce Messrs Gartons, Limited, Warring- ton, had a fine exhibit of new and regenerated breeds of seeds which have yielded some remarks ble crops and are every year increasing in favour with agriculturists, as well as securing many important champion prizes at exbibltions in all parts of the Kingdom. Mr F 0 Dutton, Horsernarket-street, War- rington, showed agricultural machinery of the most efficient and up-to-date type ' • Mr Joseph Muskett, of Higher Walton, had an exhibit of carriages, Sm. : Mr J Himsworth,l Warrington, had an attractive display of cutlery and tools • and Mrs Massey, of Acton Grange, showed flowers, plants, fruit, &c. THE OFFICIALS.

No society could be better served by its officials who from the president downwards, spare no pains to ensure perfect arrangements and a successful show. Sir Gilbert Greene!), Bart., is the president ; Mr F Stapleton-Brethertnn, vice-president; Mr S VI Gould, chairman ; Mr Samuel Davies, vice-chairman ; Mr Alfred Broome, hon. treasurer ; wtile the manifold duties of hon. secretary are capably and zealously dis- charged by Mr James Bainbridge. ===== 0002601_19120824_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
It would appear that the clerk of the weather is beginning to look with a more kindly eye upon the work of the Warrington and District Agticultural Society. For many years it was the tradition to associate more or less dismal weather with snow day, but the last two years have provided notable exceptions to the usual order of things. By the strange irony of late, however, the advantage of bright climatic condi- tions has on each occasion been counter-balanced by bad luck in other directions. Last year the sun shone brilliantly all day long, making it a delight to be on the picturesquely situated show ground at Walton, but the dislocation of railway traffic, brought about by the national strike, caused many agriculturists living at a distance to stay at home rather than risk uncertain journeys On Thursday, when the twenty-third annual show was held, the weather, after behaving so atrociously this summer, attempted to turn over a new leaf and favoured the society with a really pleasant day. The morning broke beautifully fine, although there was a cold snap in the air, and for the most part the day was one of blue skies and bright sunshine. The drawback to the show's complete success, however, came froma quarter as much beyond the control of the society as the elements themselves. Owing to the ravages of the foot- and-mouth disease, the society had to eliminate the cattle and pig sections from its schedule this year, and this,of course,meant a considerable falling-off in entries, the show being confined almost entirely to horses. To guard against the possibility of the disease being carried to the famous stock at Walton Hall, the venue of the show was changed from Walton Park, for so many years placed at the disposal of the society 1) the presideat, to the adjacent grounds of Walton Lea. In some respects the removal was no disadvantage, for the new ground is very compact and admirable adapted for show purposes. To those of the general public who make DO pretentions to expert interest in the exhibits, however, the attractiveness of the event was lessened by the withdrawal of the much appreciated privilege of inspecting the model stables and piggeries, and beautifully laid-out gardens at Walton Hall. It was therefore not surprising that the people of Warrington did not betake themselves to Walton in quite as strong force as the cheerful weather would otherwise have ensured. Nor was there as good a sprinkling as uaual of the Cheshire farmers. They were no doubt taking full advantage of the unexpected fine day to do much-needed work in the fields. But in spite of these adverse influences on the attendance, the scene around the show ring in the afternoon, where the judging of the hunters and jumpers was watched with much interest, provided a picture of colour and animation. The show as a whole well-maintained the high reputation which the society has built up, having, from very humble beginnings, taken rank as one of the best one-day exhibitions in the North of England. The entries, comparing the classes returned, were slightly fewer than in 1011, totalling 231 against 243. The falling off was chiefly noticeable in the shire section, which attracted only 59 entries as compared with 04, but there were rather more hunters, and the hunter riding classes were much better filled. The hackneys and harness classes showed a decrease, but the tradesmen's turn- outs were sliglaly better. Generally speaking, com- petition was keen and the work of judging, which began at 10 o'clock, was not completed until close upon seven in the evening. The hunters were undoubtedly the outstanding feature of the show, splendid animals being sent by many of the leading exhibitors in the country. Popular interest was chiefly focussed on the jumping competition, the negotiation of the hedge, gate and water jumps pro- viding plenty of excitement, while the skilled horse- manship displayed by the riders called forth admira- tion and applause. Incidental to the main show was the department devoted to flowers and vegetables cultivated by crittagers oa the estate, and a stroll through the tent in which their exhibits were artis- tically arranged, afforded pleasant relaxation. Visitors were also privileged to enjoy a rich musical treat, the programme submitted by the band of the Cheshire(Earl of Cheater's) Yeomanry being of a high . class character. Classical and light selections alike received musicianly and expressive treatment .and the audience was frequently moved to enthusiasm by inspiring performances of such works as the grand selection from" Lohengrin " (Wagner) and the Huy Bias" overture(Mende/ssohn). Mr. Fred L. Buckley conducted with tact and skill.

AN ACCIDENT. Considerable alarm was caused by an unfortunate incident which ocettred early in the afternoon. A horse, which was being ridden into the ring by a groom, knocked down Master T. H. Greenall, son of Mr. Henry Greenall, of Hill Cliffe, and in passing, one of the hind hoofs glanced sharply against the boy's face, inflicting a deer cut over the eye and severely bruising the face. The boy, who is nine years of age, was at once picked up in an unconscious condition and taken into a tent where he received prompt and efficient attention at the hands of members of the St. John Ambulance Corps. On regaining consciousness be was removed home in e motor car. It was reassur- ing to learn that the injuries were not regarded as serious.

===== 0002601_19120824_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME OF THE JUMPE:

FEATURES OF THE SHOW.

Although the shire section showed such a substantial falling-off numerically, the quality of the exhibits was quite up to the high stancard which one hits become accustomed to expect at Walton Show. lhe chief object of the society is, of course, to encourage local breeders and the classes were confined to the district. Mr Dan Massey's splendid grey filly Lynhurst Forest Queen, not only achieved a notable victory in her class, but secured the district champion cup and a special prize of LS. Mr James Gould, of Lymm, one of the leading breeders of heavy horses, was again pro- minent in the prize list. His Titchmarsh Baroness, which took premier honours in the class for four-3 ear. old mares and upwards without foal, and also car lied off the Shire Horse Society's gold medal, is a very useful animal, possessed of fine qualities. The second, Blossom, belonging to the executors of Mr John Blackshaw, is a characteristic mare, but lacking in size. In the novice brood mare class with foal, Mr W Blackshaw, of Hatton was awarded the first prize for a big weighty mare, and she had a pretty foil at foot. This class was one of tl,e best in the section, and the prize winner was of outstanding merit. Mr A Broome, of Preston Brook, won easily in the brood mare class with foal with a seven-year-old bay, Windmill Dnchess, and he was also awarded a silver cup for the best shire exhibit, the property of one of Sir Gilbert Greenall's tenants. She is a large weighty animal of a useful type and gained prizes at Walton, Knutsford, and Middlevvich last year. Mr Gould came second with a mare that had better feet than the winner, but she was looking a trifle stale. Mr Massey's Bramhope Venus,' which was placed third, is a beautiful six year old—in fact she was regarded by many as the prettiest in the class—but she lost in scale. The colt foals were a nice lot, and Mr Gould was an easy winner of the championship with a pretty four months old bay, Lymni Standard, an animal with particularly hard legs, which he used fairly well. Of the filly foals Mr Lewis Dodd's Rustic Uheta came first. She is a daughter of Eaton Nunsuch, the Duke of Westminster's famous sire, and makes a very pretty foal, lacking only in size. The class for yearliog colt or gelding was one of the 000 rest in the show. The yearl- ing fillies were a good lot, Tarbock Ethel, a big strong chesnut belonging to Mr A Garnett, being ticketed first. Mr John Richardson's Old Hough Carrie, a nice bay of smaller size, seas shown. Of the two-year- old geldings or fillies the outstanding exhibit was Messrs H and R Ainscough's unnamed brown filly by Admiral Bosco.

Warrington Show is noted for its hunters and on Thursday they were again the great feature, some superb animals being sent by such well-known exhibi- tors as Messrs. Drage, Gale, Wilkinson and Stokes. The Duchess of Sutherland also showed several fine speci- mens of weight-carrying hunters. The open classes were for novices, encouragement thus being given to possessors of horses which have yet to make their way in the show world. In the class for four-year-olds, to carry between 12 and 13 stone, Mr F B Wilkinson's Marcus, a particularly well furnished bay gelding, gained a well-deserved victory. The second, belonging to Mr , Jersey de Knoop, was a well-known Cheshire horse and the winner of many prizes. Mr Drage showed a workmanlike bay which looked well able to carry the driver over rough country. Some of the critics round the riip-' regretted that the Duchess of Southerland's Game Lad was not a winner but, although a very nice animal, it did not gallop so easily as the others. Of the hunters carrying between 13 and 15 stone, Mr Drase's Dauntless, a beautiful, big, roomy mare, was first, but there was little to choose between her points and those of Mr Wilkinson's Knight Errant. In the class for hunters carrying over 15 stone, the first and second again ran very closely together, but Mr Clegg's Marcus is a much better-topped horse, with rare !sack and loins, and deserved its victory over Mr Drage's Jorrocks. In the district class for brood mares, Mr W H Shiers' Beechnut 11. was again successful. It was described by the judge as a wonderfully good mare, and it has won in the best company this year, including the Royal.

The hackney section produced some wonderfully good animals. In the brood mare class Sir Lees Knowles Bart., CN.O., of Pendlebury, gained first prize with an excellent black chestnut of 13 years. She is possessed of remarkable action, and secured a first prize at Worsley on Wednesday. Mr J Davenport was wall in front with a pretty colt in the foal class. The hackneys in hand were a small class but contained horses of con- siderable merit. In the three-year-old class Mr Makeague, of Newton-le-Willows, was awarded first for a very fine mare by Polonius.' The pony brood mares were one of the strongest classes in the show, Mr F Hardwick's Jewel's Own, a handsome brown, securing the honours. The foal from Mr Hardwick's mare won in its class, Mr Joshua Ball, of Southworth Hall, being second with a very nice type of foal. Among the open classes, that for novice mares or geldings, not exceeding 14 hands, was above the average, Mr W Foster winning with one of his renowned ponies Mel-Valley's Fashion, with Messrs W and C Handforth's Lydiate Lady, a good second. Mr A .1 Willett (Stockton Heath) was third with a thirteen- hands pony by Fire boy, which was ably driven by Miss The animal has exceptionally good action but was handicapped by being lees than the others in the class. The section for mares or geldings, between 14 and 15 hands, did not contain any horses of striking merit after the winner—Grand Vim belonging to Miss Ellen Ross. In the yearling hackney class, Mr Walter Briggs won with a very fine goer by Albin Wildfire, Mr Frank Houghton (Appleton) taking the second award with a very nice-moving filly by Fakenham Sir Charles. Thejudges could not make up their minds as to the respective merits of this animal and the third prize winner, belonging to Mr E L Bent, of Newton, and had to call in Mr James Bainbridge as referee. The drat prize for the two-year old hackney was won by a very nice type of mare belonging to Mr Alfred Smith, of St Helens, but the

THE FLOWER SHOW.

A very pleasing and increasingly: popular adjunct of the agricultural show is the Walton Flower Show and Cottage Garden prize competition, in which Lady Greenall takes a special interest. From very humble beginnings it has grown year by year until it can now be regarded as thoroughly worthy of one of the prettiest villages in the district. The gardens of Walton are a beautiful picture at this time of theyear and some of their hest products were displayed with tasteful effect at the Show. Owing to the extraordinary season, from the weather point ef view, the floral section of the Show was not quite up to the standard of last year, but the vegetables showed a distinct improvement. The admiration of the visitors was chiefly attracted by some magnificient groups of greenhouse and stove plants from several of the large gardens in the district. The first prize went to P. Wilkinson, of Walton Lea, who showed a very artistic and striking group made up of choice specimens of palm, liliutn, croten, caladium and gloxinias. The attractiveness of the exhibition was increased by the special collection of cut flowers (not for competition) sent by such noted specialists as Messrs Dicksons,Ltd, Chester, Caldwell and Sons, Knntsford, Clibrans, Ltd, Altrincham, Dickson and Robinson, Manchester, R P Ker and Sons, Liverpool Mr. Henry Middlehurst, I Messrs Thomas Davies and Co. Liverpool, Sterling Seed Co., Liverpool. The work o'f judging was under- taken by Mr C Sprackling and Mr T Herbert, of Tarporley. The President of the show is Lady Greenall, the steward Mr J G Hatton, and the secre- tary, Mr James Bainbridge. The following is the prize list :- -

- - -- • PRIZE COMPETITION, DISTRICT.—For the neatest and best stocked cottage flower garden : 1, William Berry; 2, F Kippax; 3, F Hamblett. For the neatest and best stocked cottage kitchen garden : 1, F Kippax ; 2, Harry Bell ; 3, F Hamblett. For tle neatest and best stocked cottage flower garden (estateworkmen) : 1, John Kipwax ; 2, James Kippax ; 3,James Rutter and Peter Cliffe. For the neatest and best stocked kitchen garden (estate workmen) : 1, John Kippax; 2, James Kippax; 3, James Cliffe and James Rutter. Champion prize (open), for the neatest and best cottage flower and kitchen gardens: 1, James Kippax, Hatton.

TOO MANY LAWYERS. Addressing a public meeting at the Central Hall, Liverpool, on Wednesday evening, Canon Wakeford, vicar of St. Margaret's, who hss been appointed to the position of precentor of Lincoln Cathedral, said that whenever they found a State in which theorists had secured the government they found instability. One of the foreboding signs of the present time was that in the House of Commons there were 120 barristers. We were in the middle of a reshaping of England, of the largest revolution this country had ever seen, but happily it was a bloodless revolution. It was more social than political. There was a need as there bad not been for a century past for the Church to play her part in the reshaping of England. There was a groat deal more need for liberty than equality.

Pure Indian Tea is the ideal of every household. India, the home of the tea plant, has a soil and climate admirably adapted to the production of the very highest class of tea. Then the British planter, working with British brains and British capital, has the most ingenious machinery the world can devise. In every process of manufacture there is the most scrupulous cleanliness, the leaf after being gathered being un- touched by hand. Indian Tea, therefore, is pure, rich, wholesome and economical. Among its varieties can be found some to suit every palate, every purse, and the water of every district. Ask your grocer for the Indian Tea that suits YOU.
